Existence, uniqueness and propagation of chaos∑for general McKean–Vlasov and mean-field BSDEs Thanks: AS gratefully acknowledges the financial support from the Hellenic Foundation for Research and Innovation Grant No. 235 (2nd Call for H.F.R.I. Research Projects to support Post-Doctoral Researchers). ST gratefully acknowledges the financial support from the Hellenic Foundation for Research and Innovation Grant No. 05724 (3rd Call for H.F.R.I. Scholarships for PhD candidates).
Antonis Papapantoleon, Alexandros Saplaouras, Stefanos Theodorakopoulos

Abstract
. We consider backward stochastic differential equations (BSDEs) with mean-field and McKean–Vlasov interaction in their generators in a general setting, where the drivers are square–integrable martingales, typically with independent increments, and the filtrations are (possibly) stochastically discontinuous. In other words, we consider discrete- and continuous-time systems of mean-field BSDEs and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs in a unified setting. Moreover, the measure in the generator depends on all components of the solution process. We provide existence and uniqueness results for these BSDEs using adapted a priori estimates that utilize the stochastic exponential. Then, we derive propagation of chaos results for systems of particles that satisfy BSDEs, i.e. we show that the asymptotic behavior of the solutions of mean-field systems of BSDEs, as the multitude of the systems grows to infinity, converges to I.I.D. solutions of McKean–Vlasov BSDEs. We introduce a new coupling technique for showing the backward propagation of chaos, that makes repeated use of the a priori estimates, inequalities for the Wasserstein distance and the ”conservation of solutions” under different filtrations. This new approach does not require the solutions of the mean field systems to be exchangeable or symmetric, which also allows to consider cases with multiple generators. Finally, we deduce convergence rates for the propagation of chaos, under advanced integrability conditions on the solutions of the BSDEs.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
60F25, 60H20, 60G42, 60G44, 60G51

1.
Introduction

The notion of “propagation of chaos” received renewed attention, especially from the mathematical economics and mathematical finance communities, when it was used in a series of lectures by
Lasry and Lions [42]
,
Lasry and Lions [43]
,
Lasry and Lions [44]
in order to simplify the study of mean-field games.
They introduced ideas from statistical physics in the study of Nash equilibria for stochastic differential games with symmetric interactions, along with
Huang et al. [35]
,
Huang et al. [36]
.
In general, problems with a large number of players are notoriously difficult to control.
However, as statistical physics has shown us, under the appropriate assumptions, the most important being symmetry, one can study the asymptotic behavior of a system as the number of players grows to infinity much more easily.
The first instance of this phenomenon appears already in the (Strong) Law of Large Numbers, which states that, under certain conditions, the asymptotic behavior of the random empirical mean converges to the deterministic mean as the number of samples grows to infinity.
Of course, there is not a single way to mathematically express the notion that players, or agents in economics and finance, or particles in statistical physics, interact with one another and a choice must be made.
Perhaps inspired by the Law of Large Numbers, the interaction that has been extensively studied in the statistical physics literature is the one that emerges from the empirical measure of the states of the particles.
Hence, an interaction that involves the empirical measure of the states of the players in a game is called mean-field interaction.

The theory of propagation of chaos was initiated in the 1950’s by
Kac [40]
.
In the process of investigating particle system approximations for some nonlocal partial differential equations arising in thermodynamics, he made an important observation about a characteristic of large systems.
Assume that the behavior of the particles is symmetric and they interact in a weak way,
i.e.
such that its magnitude decreases inversely proportional to the size of the system, maybe due to cancelations of the contributions of different particles.
Then, if the initial positions of the particles are chaotic, here understood as independent and identically distributed, this initial state of the system could be seen asymptotically to propagate (or spread) to the other points in time, when its size grows to infinity.
This idea of propagation of chaos has been used ever since in various topics with several applications; see
Sznitman [58]
for an important treatise and
Chaintron and Diez [18]
,
Chaintron and Diez [17]
,
Jabin and Wang [37]
,
Jabin and Wang [38]
and
Malrieu [50]
for more recent results and applications.

In this work, combining the ideas above, we want to study the so-called backward propagation of chaos,
i.e.
the asymptotic behavior of solutions of mean-field systems of backward stochastic differential equations (BSDEs) in a general setting.
The backward propagation is understood as exhibiting chaotic behavior in the terminal conditions, instead of the initial conditions.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
We model the motion of
N-
particles or players in a closed system as a system of mean-field BSDEs, where the generator of the BSDE depends on the empirical measure of the solution of the whole system of BSDEs.
Let
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}:=(\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{1,N}_{\cdot},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{N,N}_{\cdot})
, where
\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y^{i,N}_{\cdot},Z^{i,N}_{\cdot},U^{i,N}_{\cdot},M^{i,N}_{\cdot}\big)
denotes the solution of the
i
-th component of the system, for
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
.
The
(Y^{i,N})_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}-
part of the solution
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
satisfies the following BSDE system:

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y^{i,N}_{t}=\xi^{i,N}+\int^{T}_{t}f^{i,N}\big(s,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},i,N}_{s},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},N}_{s})\big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{\mathbf{X}^{i}}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z^{i,N}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i,\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U^{i,N}_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{F}^{1,\dots,N},X^{i,\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M^{i,N}_{s},\end{multlined}

for
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
.
Here the modified solution equals

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},i,N}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y^{i,N}_{\cdot},Z^{i,N}_{\cdot}c^{i}_{\cdot},\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{1,\dots,N},\mathbf{X}^{i},\Theta)}(U^{i,N})_{\cdot}\big),

and collectively we denote

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},N}_{\cdot}:=(\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},1,N}_{\cdot},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},N,N}_{\cdot}).

The mean-field interaction appears in the last argument of the generator
f^{i,N}
as the respective (random) empirical measure
L^{N}
, and depends on all components of the solution,
i.e.
on
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},N}
.
The notation will be clarified at the end of this and in the next section.
However, let us quickly state the following, for
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
:

\xi^{i,N}
is the terminal condition of the BSDE for the
i
-th player,

\mathbf{X}^{i}:=(X^{i,\circ},X^{i,\natural})
is a tuple of square-integrable martingales (where one could think of them as the continuous and purely discontinuous part of a martingale) with
C^{\mathbf{X}^{i}}
associated to their predictable quadratic covariation,

M^{i,N}
is the orthogonal martingale with respect to
\mathbf{X}^{i}
,

Z^{i,N}
is the integrand of the Itō integral with respect to
X^{i,\circ}
,

U^{i,N}
is the integrand of the stochastic integral with respect to the random measure associated with
X^{i,\natural}
,
and
\Gamma
is a suitable function of
U^{i,N}
chosen from a large class.
In particular, the
\Gamma
function can be chosen as the Radon–Nikodym derivative of the stochastic integral of
U^{i,N}
with respect to the jump measure relative to the measure induced by
C^{\mathbf{X}^{i}}
, for a range of free parameters
\Theta
; see
Section

2.8
for more details.

Let us return to the interaction of the BSDE system via the empirical measure
L^{N}
.
Motivated by the earlier discussion, one expects that, by an appropriate application of the law of large numbers, the empirical measures converge to a deterministic law as
N\to\infty
, thus rendering the interactions weaker and weaker.
Obviously, the absence of the empirical measure translates the above system to
N
non-interacting equations of the same type.
In order to identify the asymptotic behavior of the
(Y^{i,N})_{i\in\mathbb{\{}1,\dots,N\}}-
part of the solution
\mathbb{S}^{N}
of the aforementioned mean-field system as
N\to\infty
, we will need a McKean–Vlasov BSDE of the form

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y_{t}=\xi+\int^{T}_{t}f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod}}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod}}_{s})\big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s},\hskip 8.5359pt\end{multlined}

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y_{\cdot},Z_{\cdot},U_{\cdot},M_{\cdot}\big)

denotes the solution of the BSDE (
1
)
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod}}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y_{\cdot},Z_{\cdot}c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{\cdot},\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{\cdot}\big).

Here, again, the notation will be clarified at the end of this section.
Let us just mention that for a random variable
R
, its law will be denoted by
\mathcal{L}(R)
.

We will first show existence and uniqueness results for mean-field systems of BSDEs of the form (
1
) and for McKean–Vlasov BDSEs of the form (
1
) in the general setting of
Papapantoleon et al. [53]
,
Papapantoleon et al. [54]
.
In other words, the driving processes are general square-integrable martingales in general filtrations that are not necessarily stochastically continuous.
Hence, we can consider discrete- and continuous-time equations, as well as continuous and purely discontinuous processes as drivers in a unified setting.

Lévy processes and more general semimartingales are natural stochastic processes when modeling phenomena in mathematical finance, because they can capture the real-world behavior of financial markets.
These models acknowledge that financial asset prices do not always move smoothly; instead, they may present abrupt changes due to unexpected events.
Moreover, the distribution of log-returns exhibits fat-tails and skews, which are captured easily by the laws of such processes.
We refer the reader to
Eberlein and Kallsen [27]
,
Cont and Tankov [22]
or
Schoutens [56]
for more details and references on models with jumps in finance.
Analogous considerations also hold for models in physics, see, for example
Metzler and Klafter [52]
.

Our general framework allows to model the driving dynamics with continuous-time processes such as Lévy processes and other semimartingales, with discrete-time processes such as random walks, as well as with superpositions of discrete- and continuous-time models, such as the so-called extended Grigelionis processes; see
Kallsen [41, Def. 2.15]
.
We also refer the interested reader to
Papapantoleon et al. [53, Section 4]
and
Papapantoleon et al. [54, Section 4]
for further examples and applications of this general setting.

Moreover, we assume that the generators
f^{i,N}
and
f
of the BSDEs can be stochastic Lipschitz, while they can also depend on the initial segments of the paths of the solution
\mathbf{Y}
,
i.e.
on
\mathbf{Y}|_{[0,\cdot]}
instead of
\mathbf{Y}_{\cdot}
.
In fact, the results of
Section

4
are equally valid in the case where the dependence in the generator comes from
\mathbf{Y}|_{[0,\cdot-]}
instead of
\mathbf{Y}|_{[0,\cdot]}
, respectively when we replace
Y_{\cdot}
with
Y_{\cdot-}
; see the notation introduced in (
2.34
).

Before we proceed to a comparison with the known literature, we owe a clarification regarding the way the terms “mean-field” and “McKean–Vlasov” are used.
Indeed, in the literature so far, the term mean-field has been used to describe a rather general class of equations, which do not necessarily share a common type of dependence,
e.g.
, the dependence of the system does not solely rely on the empirical measure.
Moreover, the term McKean–Vlasov has been occasionally used instead of the term mean-field, thus increasing the confusion around the class under consideration.
In the previous paragraphs, we have indicated the way we understand a mean-field system,
i.e.
when the dependence involves the empirical measure
L^{N}
, as well as a McKean–Vlasov equation,
i.e.
when the dependence involves the law
\mathcal{L}
.
However, the reader should keep in mind that in the articles mentioned below, not all of them understand the respective equations in the same way as used here.

The term mean-field BSDE appeared for the first time in the works of
Buckdahn et al. [11]
and
Buckdahn et al. [12]
.
The aim of the former work, which was generalized by
Laurière and Tangpi [45]
,
is to analyze the properties of a specific approximation of the solution of a mean-field BSDE,
while the aim of the latter is to provide a probabilistic interpretation of semilinear McKean–Vlasov PDEs by means of a mean-field BSDE.
Since then, the literature on both types of BSDEs has expanded along the classical directions,
e.g.
,
for controlled forward-backward dynamics see
Carmona and Delarue [14]
;
for a generator exhibiting quadratic behavior see

Hao et al. [31]
, while for one exhibiting local monotonicity see
Boufoussi and Mouchtabih [8]
;
when a type of reflection is introduced see
Briand et al. [9]
,
Li [46]
,
Li and Luo [48]
,
Briand and Hibon [10]
,
Luo [49]
;
for solutions in
L^{p}-
spaces see
Chen et al. [20]
;
when the fractional Brownian motion acts as the driver of the BSDE see
Shi et al. [57]
.
Finally, it is worth mentioning that the literature related to graphon-type systems has also been developed, see
e.g.

Bayraktar et al. [6]
and the references therein.
For applications in risk measures see
Chen et al. [19]
, while for numerical approximations based on neural networks see

Germain et al. [30]
.
Let us also mention here the very recent work of
Agarwal et al. [1]
, where a class of “anticipative” McKean–Vlasov BSDEs are studied in relation to initial margin computations for CCPs; the anticipativity means that the BSDEs depend on
Y|_{[\cdot,T]}
and not just on
Y_{\cdot}
.
See also the volumes of
Carmona and Delarue [15]
,
Carmona and Delarue [16]
.
All the above works are presented under the umbrella of Brownian drivers, wherein we abusively include the work dealing with fractional Brownian motion.
In contrast, the literature related to jump processes is rather limited.
Indeed, for the case of Lévy drivers there are, to the best of our knowledge, only the works of
Djehiche et al. [26]
, which further deals with the reflected case, and
Amini et al. [3]
.
The results presented in the current work generalize
Laurière and Tangpi [45]
, as well as
Djehiche et al. [26]
in the non-reflected case, to the case the driving martingales of the stochastic integrals are square-integrable martingales, possibly both purely discontinuous.

As a next step, we provide backward propagation of chaos results,
i.e.
we show that as the size
N
of the mean-field system of BSDEs (
1
) grows to infinity, then this system converges to
N
non-interacting McKean–Vlasov BSDEs of the form (
1
).
More precisely, for the propagation of chaos property the current framework allows for square–integrable martingales with independent increments as integrators of stochastic integrals, càdlàg predictable increasing processes as integrators of Lebesgue–Stieltjes integrals, and it also allows the dependence of the generator from the initial segments of the paths of the solution
\mathbf{Y}
.
In fact, the results of
Section

5
are also valid without change in case the dependence in the generator comes from
\mathbf{Y}|_{[0,\cdot-]}
instead of
\mathbf{Y}|_{[0,\cdot]}
; see again the notation introduced in (
2.34
).
Similarly, in the instantaneous dependence case, we can replace
Y_{\cdot}
with
Y_{\cdot-}
.

Although the propagation of chaos property has been extensively studied for (forward) stochastic differential equations (SDEs), see
e.g.
the review articles of
Chaintron and Diez [18]
,
Chaintron and Diez [17]
, only a handful of papers have been published so far on the backward propagation of chaos; see
Buckdahn et al. [11]
,
Hu et al. [34]
,
Djehiche et al. [26]
,
Buckdahn et al. [12]
,
Li and Du [47]
,
Bayraktar et al. [6]
and
Laurière and Tangpi [45]
.
Most of these works employ the coupling techniques pioneered by
Sznitman [58]
in the proof of the propagation of chaos property; some recent references can be found in
Cardaliaguet et al. [13]
and
Delarue et al. [23]
.

The present work generalizes the aforementioned articles on the backward propagation of chaos in several directions. On the one hand, we consider more general driving martingales and also combine the discrete- and continuous-time settings in a single one.
On the other hand, we introduce a new coupling technique, which allows us to obtain results under weaker conditions.
A crucial step in the proofs of backward propagation of chaos is to control the quantities that involve the solutions of the mean-field systems using quantities that involve the solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs, and thus be able to use that the latter are independent and identically distributed.
In that respect, our method combines the triangle inequality of the Wasserstein distance, the simple inequality (
2.31
) and the
a priori
estimates of
Lemma

3.1
, while the consideration of these solutions under a common probability space is made possible by the “conservation of solutions” result of
Lemma

A.8
; see also
Remark

2.3
.
Then, when we consider all the solutions of each mean-field system simultaneously, the application of the aforementioned combination of techniques enables us to finally deduce the backward propagation of chaos.

Typically in the study of backward propagation of chaos, the terminal conditions
(\xi^{i,N})_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}
for the
N
mean-field system are either assumed to be the same with the terminal conditions
(\xi^{i})_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}
of the corresponding first
N
McKean–Vlasov equations or, less commonly, exchangeable, such that the differences
\{\xi^{i,N}-\xi^{i}\}_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}
are identically distributed.
In the exchangeable case, it is then assumed that under some appropriate norms we have at the limit that
\xi^{i,N}\xrightarrow{N\rightarrow\infty}\xi^{i}
, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
.
In the present work, the method of the proofs does not require such assumptions.
Instead, it is sufficient to assume that

\displaystyle\|\xi^{i,N}-\xi^{i}\|^{2}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{|\cdot|}0,\text{ for every }i\in\mathbb{N},

and that the convergence happens at a uniform rate,
i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\xi^{i,N}-\xi^{i}\|^{2}\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{|\cdot|}0,

under an appropriate norm, which will be defined in the next section.
Moreover, in the literature, only the case where the generators remain the same for each particle and each system is studied.
However, here, we can allow the generators to vary between both particles and systems, under the condition that every generator converges to a common generator in the limit.
More specifically, a sufficient condition is the existence of suitable functions
\{(g^{i,N})_{i\in\{1,\dots,N\}}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\|f^{i,N}_{\cdot}-f_{\cdot}\|_{\infty}^{2}\leq\alpha_{\cdot}^{4}g^{i,N}_{\cdot}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathbb{P}\otimes C-
a.e., where
\alpha,A

depend on the Lipschitz condition of
(\{f^{i,N}\}_{i\in\mathscr{N}})_{N\in\mathbb{N}},f
, which satisfy

\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}g^{i,N}_{s}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}\Big]\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{|\cdot|}0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}g^{i,N}_{s}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}\Big]\xrightarrow[N\rightarrow\infty]{|\cdot|}0.

We remark that above although
f^{i,N}
were assumed stochastic,
f
was assumed deterministic. But that was only for simplicity reasons as it can also be stochastic, under the obvious modifications in the proofs, where as it is customary we can work with copies of a stochastic driver and data of a prototypic probability space.

Finally, we provide convergence rates for the propagation of chaos in case the BSDEs depend on the instantaneous value of the solutions,
i.e.
on
Y_{\cdot}
, under advanced integrability conditions for the solutions of the BSDEs.
These results generalize those found in
Laurière and Tangpi [45]
from the Brownian framework to processes with independent increments.

Laurière and Tangpi [45]
prove also that the requirements of the theorems,
i.e.
the advanced integrability conditions on the solutions, can be satisfied under an additional specific linear growth condition for the generator.
Their method of proof relies on a delicate application of Girsanov’s theorem in combination with a version of Gronwall’s inequality.
In the present work, we do not study under what conditions for the generator these integrability requirements can be satisfied, but we can say that they are trivially satisfied when the generator is bounded; see
Remark

5.16

(i)
.
The method of
Laurière and Tangpi [45]
is not directly applicable in our setting, mainly due to the extra dependence of the generator on the process
U(\cdot)
.

This paper is organized as follows:
In
Section

2
, we provide notation and preliminary results that are required for the remainder of this work.
In
Section

3
, we provide new
a priori
estimates for BSDEs utilizing the stochastic exponential.
In
Section

4
, we provide existence and uniqueness results for McKean–Vlasov BSDEs and systems of mean-field BSDEs.
In
Section

5
, we prove the propagation of chaos property for both cases, when the BSDEs depend on the initial segment of the solution, and when they only depend on the instantaneous value of the solution.
Furthermore, we provide convergence rates for the “classical” setting, when the BSDEs depend on the instantaneous value of the solution.
Finally, the appendices contain certain proofs and other auxiliary results.

1.1.
Notation

The nature of the problem and the generality of our setting require a rather cumbersome notation, whose rationale we are going to explain in this part in order to ease the effort of the reader.
Let us start with some basic information.
The set of positive natural numbers will be denoted by
\mathbb{N}
.
The natural number
N
will be used exclusively to denote the total number of particles or players.
Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, then we will use abusively the notation
\mathscr{N}:=\{1,\dots,N\}
.
The letters
p,q,m,n,d,i
and
j
will denote arbitrary positive natural numbers.
More precisely, the letters
p
,
n
and
d
, will denote the dimension of the Euclidean state space of the Itō integrator, the Euclidean state space of the spatial variable of an arbitrary integer-valued random measure, and the Euclidean state space of the stochastic integrals, respectively.
The space
\mathbb{R}^{m\times q}
consists of
m\times q-
matrices with real entries and is endowed with its usual norm,
i.e.
, for
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times q}
holds
|A|:=\textrm{Tr}[AA^{\top}]
, where
A^{\top}
is the transpose of
A
. If at any point we want to indicate that we are dealing with a matrix we may alternatively use the symbol
\|\cdot\|
.
The reader should observe that we have suppressed in the norm symbol the notational dependence in the dimension.
The elements of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
will be understood as column-vectors.
The letters
i,j
will denote the index associated with an arbitrary particle or player.
This notation will precede any other notation attributed to the same object in order for the reader to clearly understand the intended particle or player; specific examples will be given at the end of this part.
The letter
\rho
will be reserved to denote metrics.
More precisely, for a metric space
\mathscr{X}
its associated metric will be denoted by
\rho_{\mathscr{X}}
.
For product metric spaces,
e.g.
,
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{X}_{1}\times\mathscr{X}_{2}
, the associated metric will be given by

\rho_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}:=\rho_{\mathscr{X}_{1}}^{2}+\rho_{\mathscr{X}_{2}}^{2}
.

The symbols
\mathbb{P},\mathbb{E}
,
etc.
will be used in their obvious probabilistic interpretation.
Let us now declare our notational conventions.
The martingales that are intended to serve as integrators of stochastic integrals will be denoted by
X
and by
\mathbf{X}
for pairs of integrators.
More specifically, the Itō integrator will be denoted by the symbol
\circ
, while a martingale associated with an integer-valued measure will be denoted by the symbol
\natural
.
The pair
(X^{i,\circ},X^{i,\natural})
, for example, will denote the martingales that drive the stochastic integrals for the
i-
th particle or player.
The first element of the pair will be the Itō integrator, while the jumps of the second element will be associated with the integer-valued measure
\mu^{X^{\natural}}
, for which more details will be presented in the next section.
The letter
\xi
will be used to denote terminal conditions.

Since we deal with two different types of BSDEs,
i.e.
, mean-field and McKean–Vlasov, we will associate special symbols to their solutions, especially in the parts of the current work in which both types appear.
The reader already had a taste of our choices in the introduction.
Let us start with the mean-field BSDE systems.
Their solution will be denoted by an upper bar, except when it is absolutely clear, in which case we will omit it, and associated with an upper index which denotes the number of particles or players.
The solution of the mean-field system of BSDEs with
N
-players (
1
), for example, is denoted by
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
.
Let us also address the various parts of the solution.
On the one hand, when we want to focus on the elements of
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
associated with the
i-
th particle or player,
i.e.
,
(Y^{i,N},Z^{i,N},U^{i,N},M^{i,N})
, we will write
\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}
.
On the other hand, when we want to focus on a specific part of the solution,
e.g.
, say the
(Y^{i,N})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}-
part of the solution
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
, we will write
\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}
; the meaning of the symbols
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
,
\overline{\mathbb{U}}^{N}
and
\overline{\mathbb{M}}^{N}
is analogous.
Moreover, because in the generator we need to use a modification (not to be understood in the probabilistic sense) of part of the solution, we use the symbols
\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},i,N}
and
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},N}
;
i.e.
, we have annotated the upper index with the term “
mod
”.
Combining all of the above, the meaning of the symbols
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}^{\text{mod,N}}
,
\overline{\mathbb{U}}^{\text{mod,N}}
should also be clear.
In general, the double upper index “
i,N
” associates the object with the
i-
th player of the
N-
player game; for example,
\xi^{i,N}
is the terminal condition of the
i-
th player in an
N-
player game.
At this point, let us mention that we might abuse notation and rarely use the identity
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}=(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N},\overline{\mathbb{Z}}^{N},\overline{\mathbb{U}}^{N},\overline{\mathbb{M}}^{N})
, which is true up to a permutation of the elements.

Let us now turn our attention to the notation of McKean–Vlasov BSDEs.
The respective solution will be denoted by an upper wide tilde, except when it is absolutely clear, in which case we will omit it.
The solution of the McKean–Vlasov BSDE (
1
), for example, is denoted by
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}
, while
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod}}
denotes the modified part that appears within the generator.
Once a McKean–Vlasov BSDE is associated with the
i-
th particle or player, the respective solutions will be denoted by
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}
, and
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},i}
.
In particular, the terminal condition of the
i-
th particle or player will be denoted by
\xi^{i}
.
Moreover, when we need to collect the solutions of the equations associated with the first
N-
particles or players (resp. first
\hat{k}-
particles or players) for
N\in\mathbb{N}
diverging to infinity (resp. for
\hat{k}\in\mathbb{N}
fixed), we will write
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},N}
,

i.e.

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{N}:=(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{N})
and

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},N}:=(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},N})
,
(resp.
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\hat{k}}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},\hat{k}}
,

i.e.

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\hat{k}}:=(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\hat{k}})
and

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\text{mod},\hat{k}}:=(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},1},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\text{mod},\hat{k}})
).
Additionally, for the
Y-
part of
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
we will write
\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}
, resp. for the
Y-
part of
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\hat{k}}
we will write
\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}^{\hat{k}}
.
The notation for the remaining parts,
i.e.
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}^{N}
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{U}}^{N}
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{M}}^{N}
, as well as for

\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}^{\text{mod},N}
,

\widetilde{\mathbb{U}}^{\text{mod},N}
, should be obvious in view of the rationale we have adopted.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we are going to give a short overview of known results that will be useful later.
Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P})
denote a complete stochastic basis in the sense of
Jacod and Shiryaev [39, I.1.3]
.
1
1

1

Given that the filtration satisfies the usual conditions, every
\mathbb{G}-
martingale admits a càdlàg version. Accordingly, without any further explicit mention, we shall identify each martingale with its càdlàg version.

Once there is no ambiguity about the reference filtration, we are going to suppress the dependence on
\mathbb{G}
.

2.1.
Martingales

Let us denote by
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
the set of square integrable
\mathbb{G}-
martingal-

es,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q}):=\big\{L:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}{}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{q},L\text{ is a $\mathbb{G}-$martingale with }\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathbb{E}[|L_{t}|^{2}]<\infty\big\},

equipped with its usual norm,

\displaystyle\|L\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})}:=\mathbb{E}[|L_{\infty}|^{2}]=\mathbb{E}\left[{{\rm Tr}[\langle L\rangle_{\infty}^{\mathbb{G}}]}\right],

where
\langle L\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}
denotes the
\mathbb{G}-
predictable quadratic variation of
L
, which is the
\mathbb{G}-
predictable compensator of the
\mathbb{G}-
optional quadratic variation
[L]
.

Let us also define a notion of orthogonality between two square integrable martingales.
More precisely, we say that
L\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
and
Y\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{m})
are (mutually) orthogonal if and only if
\langle L,Y\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}=0
. Moreover, for a subset
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
, we denote the space of martingales orthogonal to each component of every element of
\mathcal{M}
by
\mathcal{M}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}}
,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}}

\displaystyle:=\big\{Y\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}),\ \langle Y,L\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}=0\text{ for every }L\in\mathcal{M}\big\}.

A martingale
L\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
will be called a
purely discontinuous
martingale if
L_{0}=0
and if each of its components is orthogonal to all continuous martingales of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}).

Using
[
39
, Corollary I.4.16]
we can decompose
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
as follows

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})=\mathcal{H}^{2,c}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})\oplus\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q}),

where
\mathcal{H}^{2,c}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
denotes the subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
consisting of all continuous square–integrable martingales and
\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
denotes the subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
consisting of all purely discontinuous square–integrable martingales.

Let us also provide a classical example of the decomposition of the space of square–integrable martingales; we will later expand this result to a more general setting.
Using
[
39
, Theorem I.4.18]
, any square–integrable
\mathbb{G}-
martingale
L\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
admits a unique (up to
\mathbb{P}-
indistinguishability) decomposition

(2.2)

\displaystyle L_{\cdot}=L_{0}+L^{c}_{\cdot}+L^{d}_{\cdot},

where
L^{c}_{0}=L^{d}_{0}=0
.
The process
L^{c}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,c}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
will be called the
continuous martingale part of
L
, while the process
L^{d}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{q})
will be called the
purely discontinuous martingale part of
L
.
The pair
(L^{c},L^{d})
is called the
natural pair of
L
(under
\mathbb{G}
).

2.2.
Itō stochastic integrals

Using
[
39
, Section III.6.a]
, in order to define the stochastic integral with respect to a square–integrable martingale
X\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})
, we need to select a
\mathbb{G}-
predictable, non-decreasing, and right continuous process
C^{\mathbb{G}}
with the property that

(2.3)

\displaystyle\langle X\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{\cdot}=\int_{(0,\cdot]}\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle X\rangle_{s}^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}}\mathrm{d}C_{s}^{\mathbb{G}},

where the equality is understood component-wise.
In other words,
\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle X\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C^{\mathbb{G}}}
is a predictable process with values in the set of all symmetric, non-negative definite
(p\times p)-
matrices.
Then, we define the set of integrable processes as

\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X;\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}):=\Bigg\{Z:(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}){}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{d\times p},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})),\\
\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm Tr}\left[Z_{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle X\rangle_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}}Z_{t}^{\top}\right]\mathrm{d}C_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}}\right]<\infty\Bigg\},

where
\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}
denotes the
\mathbb{G}-
predictable
\sigma-
field on
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
; see
[
39
, Definition I.2.1]
.
The associated stochastic integrals will be denoted either by
Z\cdot X
or by
\int Z_{s}\textup{d}X_{s}
.
In case we need to underline the filtration under which the Itō stochastic integral is defined, we will write either
(Z\cdot X)^{\mathbb{G}}
or
(\int Z_{s}\mathrm{d}X_{s})^{\mathbb{G}}
.
The most important relation of the stochastic integral is the following formula for its predictable quadratic variation (see
[
39
, Theorem III.6.4.c)]
)

\displaystyle\left(Z\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle X\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C^{\mathbb{G}}}Z^{\top}\right)\cdot C^{\mathbb{G}}=\langle Z\cdot X\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}.

Hence, we have the analogon of the usual Itō isometry

\displaystyle\|{Z}\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X;\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})}^{2}:=\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{\infty}{\rm Tr}\left[Z_{t}\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle X\rangle_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}}Z_{t}^{\top}\right]\mathrm{d}C_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[{\rm Tr}[\langle Z\cdot X\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{\infty}]\right].

We will denote the space of Itō stochastic integrals of processes in

\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X;\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})
with respect to
X
by
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X;\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
In particular, for
X^{c}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,c}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})
we remind the reader that, by
[
39
, Theorem III.4.5]
,
Z\cdot X^{c}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,c}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
Z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X^{c};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})
,
i.e.
,
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X^{c};\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{H}^{2,c}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

2.3.
Integrals with respect to an integer-valued random measure

Let us now expand the space, and accordingly the predictable
\sigma
–algebra, in order to construct measures that depend also on the height of the jumps of a stochastic process, that is

\displaystyle\big(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}\big):=\big(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}\otimes\mathcal{B}({\mathbb{R}^{n}})\big).

A measurable function

U:\big(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}\big)\longmapsto\left(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{B}({\mathbb{R}^{d}})\right)

is called
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}-
measurable function
and, abusing notation, the space of these functions will also be denoted by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
.
In particular, we will denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}_{+}
the space of non-negative
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}-
measurable functions.
2
2

2

We will adopt analogous notation for any non-negative measurable function, for example, for a
\sigma-
algebra
\mathcal{A}
, the set
\mathcal{A}_{+}
denotes the set of non-negative
\mathcal{A}-
measurable functions.

We say that
\mu
is a random measure if
\mu:=\{\mu\left(\omega;\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x\right)\}_{\omega\in\Omega}
is a family of non-negative measures defined on
\left(\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
, satisfying identically

\mu\left(\omega;\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)=0
.
Consider a function
U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
, then we define the process

\displaystyle U*\mu_{\cdot}(\omega):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{(0,\cdot]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}U\left(\omega,s,x\right)\mu(\omega;\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x),\textrm{ if }\int_{(0,\cdot]\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}|U\left(\omega,s,x\right)|\mu(\omega;\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)<\infty,\\
\displaystyle\infty,\textrm{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Let
X\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we associate to it the
\mathbb{G}-
optional integer-valued random measure
\mu^{X}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by its jumps via the formula

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mu^{X}(\omega;\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x):=\sum_{s>0}\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{s}(\omega)\neq 0\}}\delta_{(s,\Delta X_{s}(\omega))}(\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x),

where, for any
z\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\delta_{z}
denotes the Dirac measure at the point
z
; see also
[
39
, Proposition II.1.16]
which verifies that
\mu^{X}
is
\mathbb{G}-
optional and
\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}
–
\sigma
–finite.
Notice that
\mu^{X}(\omega;\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{0\})=0.

Moreover, for a
\mathbb{G}-
predictable stopping time
\sigma
we define the random variable

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(\omega,\sigma,x)\mu^{X}(\omega;\{\sigma\}\times\mathrm{d}x):=\\
U(\omega,\sigma(\omega),\Delta X_{\sigma(\omega)}(\omega))\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{\sigma}\neq 0,\,|U(\omega,\sigma(\omega),\Delta X_{\sigma(\omega)}(\omega))|<\infty\}}.

Since
X\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the
\mathbb{G}-
compensator of
\mu^{X}
under
\mathbb{P}
exists, see

[
39
, Theorem II.1.8]
.
This is the unique, up to a
\mathbb{P}-
null set,
\mathbb{G}-
predictable random measure
\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that it holds for every
U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}_{+}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[U*\mu^{X}_{\infty}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[U*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}_{\infty}\right].

Let
U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}_{+}
and consider a
\mathbb{G}-
predictable time
\sigma
, whose graph
3
3

3

See
[
39
, Notation I.1.22]
and the comments afterwards for the distinction between the graph of the stopping time
\sigma
compared to the graph of the mapping
\sigma:\Omega\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
.
Namely, the former is the restriction on
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
of the latter.
is denoted by
\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket
.
We define the random variable

(2.5)

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{\sigma}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}:=(U\mathds{1}_{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket})*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}_{\infty}.

Using
[
39
, Property II.1.11]
, we have

(2.6)

\displaystyle\widehat{U}_{\sigma}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}=\mathbb{E}\big[(U\mathds{1}_{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket})*\mu^{X}_{\infty}\big|\mathcal{G}_{\sigma-}\big]

In particular, for
U=1
we define

(2.7)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\sigma}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}(\omega):=\widehat{1}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}_{\sigma}=\mathds{1}_{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}_{\infty}.

In order to define the stochastic integral of a function
U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
with respect to the
\mathbb{G}-
compensated integer-valued random measure

{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\!:=\mu^{X}-\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}
, we will consider the following class

G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigg\{U:\big(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}\big){}\longrightarrow\big(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big),\\
\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{t>0}\left|U(t,\Delta X_{t})\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{t}\neq 0\}}-\widehat{U}_{t}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\right|^{2}\bigg]<\infty\bigg\}.

Any element of
G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be associated to an element of
\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, uniquely up to
\mathbb{P}-
indisting-

uishability, via

\displaystyle G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d})\ni U{}\longmapsto U\star{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

see
[
39
, Definition II.1.27, Proposition II.1.33.a]
and
He et al. [32, Theorem XI.11.21]
.
We call
U\star{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}
the
stochastic integral of
U
with respect to
{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}
.
Let us point out that for an arbitrary function of
G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d})
the two processes
U*(\mu^{X}-\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)})
and
U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}
are not equal.
4
4

4

In the finite-variation case the two processes are identical.

We will make use of the following notation for the space of stochastic integrals with respect to
{\widetilde{\mu}}^{X}
which are square integrable martingales

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\left\{U\star{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)},\ U\in G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}.

Moreover, by
[
39
, Theorem II.1.33]
or
[
32
, Theorem 11.21]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\langle U\star{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{\infty}\right]<\infty\text{ if and only if }U\in G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

which enables us to define the following more convenient space

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\left\{U:\big(\widetilde{\Omega},\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}\big){}\longrightarrow\big(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\big),\ \mathbb{E}\left[\text{Tr}\Big[\langle U\star{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{\infty}\Big]\right]<\infty\right\},

and we emphasize that we have the direct identification

\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d})=G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Let us finish this subsection with the following useful formulas

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\text{Tr}\langle U\star{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{\infty}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t>0}\left|U(t,\Delta X_{t})\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X_{t}\neq 0\}}-\widehat{U}_{t}^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t>0}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(t,x)\,\mu^{X}(t,\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(t,x)\,\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}(t,\mathrm{d}x)\right|^{2}\right].

2.4.
Doléans-Dade measure and disintegration

Let
\mu
be a random measure, then we define on
\big(\widetilde{\Omega},\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\big)
its Doléans-Dade measure
M_{\mu}
as follows

\displaystyle M_{\mu}(\mathds{1}_{A}):=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}_{A}*\mu_{\infty}\right],

for every
A\in\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
In particular, for a given
X\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with non-trivial purely discontinuous part,
M_{\mu^{X}}
is
\sigma
–integrable with respect to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
.
Therefore, we can define for every
W\in\big(\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\big)_{+}
its
M_{\mu^{X}}-
conditional expectation with respect to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
, which we denote by
M_{\mu^{X}}[W|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}]
.
Furthermore, since
\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}
is the
\mathbb{G}-
compensator of
\mu^{X}
under
\mathbb{P}
, by definition, we have for every
W\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}_{+}

(2.8)

\displaystyle M_{\mu^{X}}(W)=M_{\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}}(W).

Let us denote by
|I|
the map
\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni x\mapsto|x|+\mathds{1}_{\{0\}}(x)\in(0,\infty)
.
Then, using the facts that
M_{\mu^{X}}(|I|^{2})<\infty
,
M_{\mu^{X}}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{0\})=0=M_{\nu^{X}}(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\{0\})
and that
|I|^{2}
belongs to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}_{+}
, we can use in the sequel the finite measures
|I|^{2}*\mu^{X}
, resp.
|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}
, as well as their associated Doléans–Dade measures, for which identically holds

\displaystyle M_{|I|^{2}*\mu^{X}}(\mathds{1}_{A})=M_{\mu^{X}}\left(|I|^{2}\mathds{1}_{A}\right),

\displaystyle\text{ resp. }M_{|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}}(\mathds{1}_{A})=M_{\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}}\left(|I|^{2}\mathds{1}_{A}\right),

for every
A\in\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then, for every predictable, increasing process
C
such that
|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)}\ll C

\mathbb{P}
–a.s., we get from
[
32
, Theorem 5.14]
that
\mathbb{P}\otimes(|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X)})\ll\mathbb{P}\otimes C
.

Consider a pair of martingales
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and define

(2.9)

\displaystyle C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}:=\text{Tr}\left[\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}\right]+|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}.

Using the Kunita–Watanabe inequality in conjunction with the Young inequality, following the arguments of
[
39
, Proposition II.2.9]
we can easily verify that
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
possesses the property described in (
2.3
) with respect to
X^{\circ}
.
We underline that
\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}}
is a
\mathbb{G}-
predictable process with values in the set of all symmetric, non-negative definite
(p\times p)-
matrices.
Moreover, let us define the square-root process

(2.10)

c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}:=\bigg(\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

with values in the set of symmetric, non-negative definite
(p\times p)-
matrices.
Using the diagonalization property of these matrices and the results of
Azoff [4]
, one can easily show that
c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
will also be a
\mathbb{G}-
predictable process with values in the set of all symmetric, non-negative definite
(p\times p)-
matrices.
Additionally, one notices that we can disintegrate

\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}
,
i.e.
, we can determine kernels

K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}:\left(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}\right){}\longrightarrow\mathcal{R}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
, where
\mathcal{R}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)
are the Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that

(2.11)

\displaystyle\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\omega,\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)=K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}(\omega,t,\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\omega).

The kernels
K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
are
\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}-
unique, as can be deduced by a straightforward Dynkin class argument.

At this point, let us make an immediate, yet crucial, remark for the current work.

Remark 2.1
.

Assuming that
\mathbb{G}
is immersed in
\mathbb{H}
,
i.e.
,
\mathbb{H}
is a filtration such that
\mathcal{G}_{t}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{t}
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and, additionally, it possesses the property that every
\mathbb{G}-
martingale is an
\mathbb{H}-
martingale, then
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
and
C^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}
are indistinguishable.
Indeed, one immediately has that

\displaystyle\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}-\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{H}}=(\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}-X^{\circ}\cdot\left(X^{\circ}\right)^{\top})+(X^{\circ}\cdot\left(X^{\circ}\right)^{\top}-\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{H}})

is an
\mathbb{H}-
martingale,
\mathbb{H}-
predictable and of finite variation.
Hence, since its initial value is
0
, it is the zero martingale, which proves that
\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}
and
\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{H}}
are indistinguishable.
One can argue analogously for the processes
|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}
and
|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
.
In other words, we are allowed to interchange the filtration symbol in the notation of (
2.9
), or even to omit it.

2.5.
Orthogonal decompositions

Let us now state the decomposition results that will be used to solve the BSDEs of interest; for more details, we refer to
Papapantoleon et al. [53, Section 2.2.1]
.

Let
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}[\Delta X^{\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0
.
Using this assumption, we get that for
Y^{1}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{p})
and
Y^{2}\in\mathcal{K}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R})
it holds that
\langle Y^{1},Y^{2}\rangle=0
; see
e.g.

Cohen and Elliott [21, Theorem 13.3.16]
.
Now, an arbitrary element of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R})
, say
Y
, can be decomposed as follows

(2.12)

\displaystyle Y=Y_{0}+Z\cdot{X}^{\circ}+U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{X^{\natural}}+{N},

for some
(Z,U)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},{X}^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R})
and
N\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R})
such that

\langle{X}^{\circ},N\rangle=0
and
M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}\big[\Delta{N}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}\big]=0
.
Moreover, this decomposition is unique, up to indistinguishability.
The interested reader may find the proof at
[
53
, Proposition 2.5]
.

Then we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\big(\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})\oplus\mathcal{K}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}}.

Subsequently, we have the following description for
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is
[
53
, Proposition 2.6]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with

M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}[\Delta X^{\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})=\big\{L\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \langle X^{\circ},L\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}=0\text{ and }M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}[\Delta L|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0\big\}.

Moreover, the space
\big(\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\big)
is closed.

Summing up the previous results, we arrive at the decomposition result that is going to dictate the structure of the BSDEs in our setting

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{K}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\oplus\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where each of the spaces appearing in the equality above is closed.

Remark 2.3
.

In
Lemma

A.8
we essentially show that for
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}[\Delta X^{\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0
, and a filtration
\mathbb{H}
such that
\mathbb{G}
is immersed in
\mathbb{H}
, the above decomposition for elements of
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is conserved with respect to the corresponding spaces of
\mathbb{H}
.

2.6.
Stochastic exponential

Let
A
be a finite variation, càdlàg process, and define the process

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(A)_{\cdot}:=\mathrm{e}^{A_{\cdot}}\prod_{s\leq\cdot}\frac{1+\Delta A_{s}}{\mathrm{e}^{\Delta A_{s}}},

which is called the stochastic exponential of
A
.
Using the trivial inequalities
0\leq 1+x\leq\mathrm{e}^{x}
, for all
x\geq-1
, and the usual properties of the jumps of finite variation processes, we can easily see that the above process is well defined, adapted, càdlàg and locally bounded.
The main functionality of the above process is that it satisfies the SDE

(2.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(A)_{t}=1+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}(A)_{s-}\mathrm{d}A_{s}.

This fact is proved using Itō’s formula, and yields that
\mathcal{E}(A)
also has finite variation; see
e.g.

[
21
, Section 15.1]
.

In the sequel, we will need additional properties for a process expressed as a stochastic exponential; therefore, we collect them in the next lemma.
In order to simplify notation, we adopt the following convention: whenever we write
\Delta A\neq-1
, we mean that the set
\{\Delta A_{t}=-1\text{ for some }t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
is evanescent.
Analogous will be the understanding for
\Delta A\geq a
, for any
a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, as well as for
\mathcal{E}(A)\neq 0
,
etc
.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
A
be a càdlàg process of finite variation.

(i)

If
\Delta A\neq-1
, then
\mathcal{E}(A)\neq 0
.

(ii)

If
\Delta A\neq-1
, then

\mathcal{E}(A)^{-1}=\mathcal{E}\left(-\overline{A}\right)
, where
\overline{A}_{\cdot}:=A_{\cdot}-\sum_{s\leq\cdot}\frac{(\Delta A_{s})^{2}}{(1+\Delta A_{s})}
.

(iii)

If
\Delta A\geq-1
,
then
0\leq\mathcal{E}(A)_{\cdot}\leq\mathrm{e}^{A_{\cdot}}
.
Actually,
\Delta A>-1
if and only if
\mathcal{E}(A)>0
.

(iv)

If
A
is non-decreasing, then
\mathcal{E}(A)
is non-decreasing.
If
A
is non-increasing with
\Delta A>-1
, then
\mathcal{E}(A)
is non-increasing.

(v)

If
B
is another finite variation process, then we have the identity

\mathcal{E}(A)\mathcal{E}(B)=\mathcal{E}(A+B+[A,B]),

where
[A,B]_{\cdot}:=\sum_{s\leq\cdot}\Delta A_{s}\Delta B_{s}
.

(vi)

Let
\overline{A}
be as defined in
(ii)
, then we have the identities

\displaystyle\Delta\overline{A}_{\cdot}=\frac{\Delta A_{\cdot}}{1+\Delta A_{\cdot}}\ \text{ and }\ \overline{A}_{\cdot}=A_{0}+\int_{0}^{\cdot}\frac{1}{1+\Delta A_{s}}\mathrm{d}A_{s}.

Moreover, if
A
is non-decreasing, then
\mathcal{E}(-\overline{A})
is non-increasing.

(vii)

Let
\gamma,\delta\geq 0
and
\overline{A}
as defined in
(ii)
.
Define

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}_{\cdot}:=\delta A_{\cdot}-\overline{\gamma A}_{\cdot}-[\delta A,\overline{\gamma A}]_{\cdot}.

Then, from
(ii)
and
(v)
it trivially holds

\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})=\mathcal{E}(\delta A)\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\Delta\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}_{\cdot}=\frac{\Delta((\delta-\gamma)A)_{\cdot}}{1+\Delta(\gamma A)_{\cdot}}\ \text{ and }\ \widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}_{\cdot}=A_{0}+\int_{0}^{\cdot}\frac{1}{1+\Delta(\gamma A)_{s}}\mathrm{d}((\delta-\gamma)A)_{s}.

If
A
is non-decreasing, then
\Delta(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})>-1
.

(viii)

Let
A
be non-decreasing with
\Delta A\leq\Phi
, for some
\Phi\geq 0
.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}\leq\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{t-}.

Proof.

The above properties are fairly standard and follow from relatively simple calculations; one may consult
[
21
, Section 15.1]
for
(i)
–
(v)
.
Regarding the other properties, let
\overline{A}
be understood as in
(ii)
.

One immediately derives
(vi)
and
(vii)
once the respective continuous and discontinuous parts are compared.
Indeed, regarding
(vi)
we have, on the one hand, that the continuous part of
\overline{A}
is
A^{c}
, while
\Delta\overline{A}_{\cdot}=\frac{(\Delta A_{\cdot})^{2}}{1+\Delta A_{\cdot}}
.
On the other hand,

\displaystyle\left(\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{1+\Delta A_{s}}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\right)^{c}=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{1+\Delta A_{s}}\mathrm{d}A_{s}^{c}=A^{c}_{t},

where the second equality is true because the process
\Delta A
is non-zero only countably many times and the measure associated to
A^{c}
is atomless.
Moreover, when one compares the respective jump processes for every
t\geq 0

\displaystyle\Delta\overline{A}_{t}=\Delta A_{t}-\frac{(\Delta A_{t})^{2}}{1+\Delta A_{t}}=\frac{\Delta A_{t}}{1+\Delta A_{t}}=\Delta\left(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\frac{1}{1+\Delta A_{s}}\mathrm{d}A_{s}\right)_{t}.

Let us now verify that
\mathcal{E}(-\overline{A})
is non-increasing.
Firstly, let us observe that
\mathcal{E}(-\overline{A})_{-}
is positive.
Indeed, from the previous arguments

\displaystyle 0\leq\Delta(\overline{A})<1\Leftrightarrow-1<\Delta(-\overline{A})\leq 0,

which from
(iii)
yields the desired property.
Then,
\overline{A}
is non-decreasing, or equivalently
-\overline{A}
is non-increasing, a property inherited from
A
.
Now we can conclude in view of (
2.14
).

Let us focus now on
(vii)
.
On the one hand, the continuous parts of
\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}
and
(\delta-\gamma)A
are identical.
On the other hand, we have for their discontinuous parts

\displaystyle\Delta(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})

\displaystyle=\Delta(\delta A)-\Delta(\overline{\gamma A})-\Delta(\delta A)\Delta(\overline{\gamma A})

\displaystyle=\Delta(\delta A)-\frac{\Delta(\gamma A)}{1+\Delta(\gamma A)}-\Delta(\delta A)\frac{\Delta(\gamma A)}{1+\Delta(\gamma A)}

(2.15)

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta((\delta-\gamma)A)}{1+\Delta(\gamma A)}.

Using the fact that
\Delta A
is non-zero only countably many times, we can conclude as before that

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}_{\cdot}=A_{0}+\int_{0}^{\cdot}\frac{1}{1+\Delta(\gamma A)_{s}}\mathrm{d}\big((\delta-\gamma)A\big)_{s}.

Finally, we only need to verify that
\Delta(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})>-1
, which is trivial in view of the following equivalences

-1<\Delta(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})\overset{\eqref{jump_tildeA}}{=}\frac{\Delta\big((\delta-\gamma)A\big)}{1+\Delta(\gamma A)}\Longleftrightarrow-1-\Delta(\gamma A)<\Delta\big((\delta-\gamma)A\big)\Longleftrightarrow-1<\Delta(\delta A).

We conclude with the proof of
(viii)
.
Using
(vi)
for the jumps of
\overline{\gamma A}
, yields

-1<\Delta(-\overline{\gamma A})\leq 0,

which further implies that
\mathcal{E}(-\overline{\gamma A})>0
; see
(iii)
.
Then,
(vi)
,

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{item:StochExp2}}{=}\int_{t}^{T}\mathcal{E}(-\overline{\gamma A})_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}\overset{\ref{item:StochExp6}}{=}\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{t}^{T}(1+\Delta(\gamma A_{s}))\mathcal{E}(-\overline{\gamma A})_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}(\overline{\gamma A})_{s}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\int_{t}^{T}\mathcal{E}(-\overline{\gamma A})_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}(-\overline{\gamma A})_{s}\overset{\eqref{equation:stochexp}}{=}-\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\mathcal{E}(-\overline{\gamma A})\big|_{t}^{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}\big|_{T}^{t}\leq\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{t-},

where we used the fact that
\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{\cdot}
is non-increasing.
∎

2.7.
Norms and spaces

We will largely follow the notation for norms and spaces of stochastic processes of
Papapantoleon et al. [53, Section 2.3]
.
However, we will need to additionally keep track of the filtration under which we are working, since later many filtrations will appear in our framework.
We inform the reader that we will, essentially, omit the notational dependence on the time horizon
T
, since this will be fixed at the beginning of each subsequent section.

Let
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with

M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}[\Delta X^{\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0
, and consider
A,C:(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}){}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+}))
càdlàg and increasing.
The following spaces will appear in the analysis throughout this article, for
\beta\geq 0
and
T
a
\mathbb{G}-
stopping time:

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigg\{\xi\text{ is an }\mathbb{R}^{d}-\text{valued, }\mathcal{G}_{T}-\text{random variable s.t.},\\
\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{E}(\beta A)_{T-}|\xi|^{2}\right]<\infty\bigg\},\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigg\{M\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
\|M\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\beta A)_{t-}\,\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}\big[\langle M\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}\big]_{t}\bigg]<\infty\bigg\},\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigg\{\phi\text{ is an }\mathbb{R}^{d}-\text{valued, }\mathbb{G}-\text{optional process s.t.}\\
\|\phi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\beta A)_{t-}|\phi|_{t}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}C_{t}\bigg]<\infty\bigg\},\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigg\{\phi\text{ is an }\mathbb{R}^{d}-\text{valued,}\hskip 2.84544pt\mathbb{G}-\text{optional process s.t.}\\
\|\phi\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big\{\mathcal{E}(\beta A)_{t-}|\phi|_{t}^{2}\big\}\Big]<\infty\bigg\},\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}):=\bigg\{Z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}),\\
\|Z\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})}:=\|Z\cdot X^{\circ}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty\bigg\},\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\bigg\{U\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
\|U\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\|U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{X^{\natural}}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty\bigg\},\end{multlined}

and

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})\\
\text{ with }\|M\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\|M\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}.\end{multlined}

Remark 2.5
.

Although the domain of the paths of stochastic processes we work with is the whole
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the quantities we presented above, which are tailor made for our problem, exist only up to time
T
.
This creates the usual situation where the objects of our spaces are in fact equivalent classes; this will always be implicitly assumed.
Hence, we are able to assume, whenever needed, that any object of, for example,
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})
is constant on the interval
[T,\infty)
.
Later on we will exploit this property in the proofs of the existence and uniqueness theorems.

Let us now present the norms for the solutions of the BSDEs in our setting.
Let

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}(Y,Z,U,M)\\
\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})\times\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{multlined}

then we define

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\|\left(Y,Z,U,M\right)\|^{2}_{\star,\beta,\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}}:=\\
\|Y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|Z\cdot X^{\circ}+U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{X^{\natural}}+M\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}.\end{multlined}

In some results which we will present, the
Y-
part of the solution will be required to live in a space smaller than
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
That is, it should live in

\displaystyle\mathscr{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\big\{Y\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d}):\|\alpha Y\|_{\mathbb{H}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty\big\}

with

(2.18)

\displaystyle\|\cdot\|^{2}_{\mathscr{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,C,\alpha;\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\|\cdot\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\alpha\cdot\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Moreover, for

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}(Y,Z,U,M)\\
\in\mathscr{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})\times\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\end{multlined}

we define the following norm for the solution

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\|\left(Y,Z,U,M\right)\|^{2}_{\text{\scalebox{0.5}{$\blacksquare$}},\beta,\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}}:=\\
\|Y\|^{2}_{\mathscr{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|Z\cdot X^{\circ}+U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{X^{\natural}}+M\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\end{multlined}

In particular for the martingale part, we underline the immediate equality

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\|Z\cdot X^{\circ}+U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{X^{\natural}}+M\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\\
\|Z\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})}+\|U\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|M\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})},\end{multlined}

which is true because of the assumption
M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}[\Delta X^{\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0
(see also
[
21
, Theorem 13.3.16]
), in conjunction with the fact that
M\in\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Later, in the course of computations, we will need to rewrite the norms associated with the spaces
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,

X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})
and
\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
in terms of Lebesgue–Stieltjes integrals with respect to
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
, for
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
as defined in (
2.9
); one may consult
[
53
, Lemma 2.13]
for the details.
Hence, for
(Z,U)\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})

\times\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X\natural};\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

(2.23)

\displaystyle\|Z\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}({\beta}A)_{s-}|Z_{s}c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}|^{2}\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\bigg],

where

c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
was defined in (
2.10
), and

(2.24)

\displaystyle\|U\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,\mu^{X\natural};\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}({\beta}A)_{s-}\left(\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{s}(\cdot)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\right)^{2}\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\bigg],

where

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\Big(\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{s}(\omega;\cdot)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega)\Big)^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\big|U(\omega,s,x)-\widehat{U}_{s}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}(\omega)\big|^{2}K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega,\mathrm{d}x)\\
+\big(1-\zeta^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{s}(\omega)\big)\Delta C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega)\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(\omega,s,x)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega,\mathrm{d}x)\right|^{2},\end{multlined}

with
K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
satisfying (
2.11
),
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\omega,\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)=K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}(\omega,t,\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\omega),

and
\zeta^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{\cdot}=1_{\llbracket\sigma\rrbracket}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{\cdot}
, which was defined in (
2.7
).

Remark 2.6
.

In order to simplify the notation whenever possible, if we consider one of the aforementioned spaces for
\beta=0
, then we will omit
0
.
As a result, the dependence on the process
A
is redundant, hence we will also omit the process
A
from the notation of the respective space.
As an example,
\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{T};\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the space
\mathbb{L}^{2}_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which is the classical Lebesgue space.

2.8.
The
\Gamma
function

The integrand in the stochastic integral with respect to the purely-discontinuous martingale
\widetilde{\mu}^{X^{\natural}}
is a suitable process
U
, and the driver of the BSDE
f
will, obviously, depend on this process.
However, in the present work, we cannot simply require that
f
is Lipscitz in this argument with respect to the
\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|
-norm defined in (
2.7
), as done
e.g.
in
Papapantoleon et al.

[
53
,
54
]
, because this norm depends on the filtration
\mathbb{G}
.
Hence, motivated by applications and connections to PDEs, see
Remark

2.8

(iii)
, we define a composition of functions, called the
\Gamma
function, with a free parameter
\Theta
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
let
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
be as defined in (
2.9
)
and
K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
that satisfies (
2.11
).
Additionally, let
\Theta
be an
\mathbb{R}
–valued,
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}-
measurable function such that
|\Theta|\leq|I|
; the reader may recall that
|I|(x):=|x|+\mathds{1}_{\{0\}}(x)
.
Define the process

\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}:\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d}){}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that, for every
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, holds

\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{s}(\omega):=\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left(U(\omega,s,x)-\widehat{U}_{s}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\omega)\right)\left(\Theta(\omega,s,x)-\widehat{\Theta}_{s}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\omega)\right)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega,\mathrm{d}x)\\
\hskip-80.00012pt+(1-\zeta^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{s}(\omega))\Delta C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega)\\
\times\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(\omega,s,x)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega,\mathrm{d}x)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Theta(\omega,s,x)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}(\omega,\mathrm{d}x).

Remark 2.8
.

(i)

Given that
X^{\natural}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it is immediate that the process
\Theta\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R})
.
Therefore, for any
U\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the process
\Gamma
is well-defined
\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}-
a.e.

(ii)

One would expect in
\Gamma
a notational dependence on the kernel.
However,
K_{1}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})},K_{2}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
satisfying (
2.11
) and
U\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so we have
\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{s}=\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{s},

\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}-
a.e.; here, implicitly, the left hand side is defined with respect to
K_{1}
and the right hand side with respect to
K_{2}
.
In that way, we have uniqueness of the kernels that satisfy (
2.11
).
Since in the respective computations all the equalities appearing will be taken under
\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
, we have suppressed the notational dependence on the kernels.

(iii)

The choice of
\Gamma
was based on, and inspired by, applications.
The reader may recall, for example, the connection between BSDEs and partial integro-differential equations, and the special structure that is required for the generator, see
e.g.

Barles et al. [5]
or
Delong [24, Section 4.2]
.
Moreover, one can easily verify that
\Gamma
is equal to Radon–Nikodym derivative

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})},\Theta\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}}.

The next lemma can be viewed as a version of the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and, essentially, justifies the definition of the process
\Gamma
.
In other words, the function
\Gamma
is Lipschitz in the sense described below.
However, note that the inner characteristics of the function
\Gamma
play no part in what follows in the other sections. If one can prove that results similar to
Lemma

2.9
and
Lemma

A.5
hold, then the rest will remain valid, under the appropriate modifications.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

\Theta\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
be an
\mathbb{R}
–valued function such that
|\Theta|\leq|I|
.
Then, for every
U^{1},U^{2}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\big|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{t}(\omega)-\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U^{2})_{t}(\omega)\big|^{2}\\
\leq 2\left(\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U^{1}_{t}(\omega;\cdot)-U^{2}_{t}(\omega;\cdot)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\omega)\right)^{2},\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}-\text{a.e.}

Proof.

Let
\delta U:=U^{1}-U^{2}
then, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, we get

\displaystyle\big|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{t}-\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U^{2})_{t}\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq 2\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big[\delta U_{t}(x)-\widehat{\delta U}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}\Big]\hskip 2.84544pt\left[\Theta_{t}(x)-\widehat{\Theta}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}\right]\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\right|^{2}\\
+2(1-\zeta^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{t})^{2}\big(\Delta C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}\big)^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\delta U_{t}(x)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\right|^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Theta_{t}(x)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\right|^{2}\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq 2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\delta U_{t}(x)-\widehat{\delta U}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}\right|^{2}\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\hskip 2.84544pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left|\Theta_{t}(x)-\widehat{\Theta}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}\right|^{2}\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\\
+2(1-\zeta^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{t})^{2}\big(\Delta C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}\big)^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\delta U_{t}(x)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\right|^{2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Theta_{t}(x)\,K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\mathrm{d}x)\right|^{2}\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\leq 2\Big(\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U^{2}_{t}(\omega\hskip 2.84544pt;\cdot)-U^{1}_{t}(\omega\hskip 2.84544pt;\cdot)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\omega)\Big)^{2}\Big(\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Theta_{t}(\cdot)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\omega)\Big)^{2},\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}-\text{a.e}.

Using
[
32
, 11.21 Theorem, Part 3)]
,
|\Theta|\leq|I|
and
X^{\natural}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

\displaystyle\langle{\Theta}\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{\cdot}=|\Theta|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{\cdot}-\sum_{s\leq\cdot}\big|\widehat{\Theta}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\big|^{2}.

Hence, for every
s,u\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
with
s<u
, we get

\langle\Theta\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{u}-\langle\Theta\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{s}=|\Theta|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{u}-|\Theta|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{s}-\sum_{s<r\leq u}\big|\widehat{\Theta}^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{r}\big|^{2}\\
\leq|\Theta|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{u}-|\Theta|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{s}=\left(|\Theta|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\rrbracket s,u\rrbracket}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{\infty}\\
\leq\left(|I|^{2}\mathds{1}_{\rrbracket s,u\rrbracket}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{\infty}=|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{u}-|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}_{s},\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbb{P}-\text{a.e}.

By a straightforward monotone class argument, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\langle\Theta\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}\leq\mathrm{d}\big(|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\big),\ \mathbb{P}-\text{a.e}.

Therefore, using the above, we get

\displaystyle\left(\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\Theta_{t}(\cdot)\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{t}(\omega)\right)^{2}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\langle\Theta\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}}{\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}\big(|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\big)}{\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}}\leq 1,\hskip 5.69046pt\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}-\text{a.e.},

and the proof is thus complete.
∎

2.9.
Wasserstein distance

Let
\mathbb{X}
be a Polish space endowed with a metric
\rho
, then we denote by
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X})
the space of probability measures on
(\mathbb{X},\rho)
.
Moreover, for every real
r\in[1,\infty)
, we define the probability measures on
\mathbb{X}
with finite
r
moment to be
