\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X}):=\left\{\mu:\int_{\mathbb{X}}\rho(x_{0},x)^{r}\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty\right\},\text{ for some }x_{0}\in\mathbb{X}.

By the triangle inequality and the fact that we consider probability (
i.e.
finite) measures, it is immediate that the space
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
is independent of the choice of
x_{0}
.
The space
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X})
is equipped with the usual weak topology,
6
6

6

We use the term as probabilists do,
i.e.
, the topological dual space of the set of probability measures is the set of continuous and bounded functions defined on
\mathbb{X}
. In other words, from the point of view of functional analysis, this is the weak
{}^{*}-
topology.
which we denote by
\mathcal{T}
.
Let us recall the form of the elements for the usual basis of
\mathcal{T}
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
f:\mathbb{X}{}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be any continuous and bounded function (i.e.
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{X})
), then we denote by
I^{f}:\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X}){}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the function where

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X})\ni\mu\longmapsto\int_{\mathbb{X}}f(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\in\mathbb{R}.

We can now give a description of the usual basis of
\mathcal{T}

(2.27)

\displaystyle\textbf{B}(\mathcal{T}):=\left\{\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}\left(I^{f_{i}}\right)^{-1}(A_{i}):m\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{X}),A_{i}\text{ open sets of }\mathbb{R}\right\}.

The weak topology is metrizable and, in fact,
(\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X}),\mathcal{T})
is a Polish space; see
Aliprantis and Border [2, 15.15 Theorem]
.
Moreover, it is well-known, see
e.g.

[
2
, 15.3 Theorem]
, that
a sequence of probability measures
\{\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to a probability measure
\mu

if and only if for every bounded and continuous function
f
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}f(x)\,\mu_{m}(\mathrm{d}x)\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{}\int_{\mathbb{X}}f(x)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x).

On
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
we can define an even stronger mode of convergence, that allows for more functions to be tested.
We will simply call it weak convergence in
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
.
This mode says that a sequence of probability measures
\{\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly in
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
to a probability measure
\mu

if and only if for every continuous function
f
such that
|f(x)|\leq C(1+\rho(x_{0},x)^{r})
, where
C:=C(f)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

(2.28)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}f(x)\,\mu_{m}(\mathrm{d}x)\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{}\int_{\mathbb{X}}f(x)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x).

Of course, as before, it is immediate that in the above definition it does not matter which
x_{0}
we choose.

Now, one can easily see that the topology produced by this mode of convergence has a natural base, similar to the one of the weak topology. For the purpose of writing it down let us give the following definition.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
r\in[1,\infty)
and
f:\mathbb{X}{}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
any continuous function such that
|f(x)|\leq C(1+\rho(x_{0},x)^{r})
, where
C:=C(f)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then we denote by
I^{f}_{r}:\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X}){}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the function where

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})\ni\mu\longmapsto\int_{\mathbb{X}}f(x)\mu(\mathrm{d}x)\in\mathbb{R}.

Hence, similar to before, the natural base for the topology
\mathcal{T}_{r}
of the weak convergence in
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X})_{r}
is

(2.29)

\displaystyle\textbf{B}_{r}(\mathcal{T}_{r}):=\Bigg\{\bigcap_{i=1}^{m}\left(I^{f_{i}}_{r}\right)^{-1}(A_{i}):m\in\mathbb{N},f_{i}\in C(\mathbb{X})\hskip 2.84544pt\text{such that}

\displaystyle|f_{i}(x)|\leq C(f_{i})(1+\rho(x_{0},x)^{r}),A_{i}\text{ open sets of }\mathbb{R}\Bigg\}.

Additionally,
\mathcal{T}_{r}

is metrizable from a metric with nice properties.
This metric is called the Wasserstein distance of order
r
.
More precisely, given two probability measures
\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
we define the Wasserstein distance of order
r
between them to be

(2.30)

\mathcal{W}_{r,\rho}^{r}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\left\{\int_{\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}}\rho(x,y)^{r}\,\pi(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y)\right\},

where
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
are the probability measures on
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
with marginals
\mu
and
\nu
.
The interested reader may consult
Villani [60, Theorem 6.9]
for the fact that
\mathcal{W}_{r,\rho}
metrizes
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
.
Moreover,
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
with this mode of convergence is a Polish space, as can be seen from
[
60
, Theorem 6.18]
. So, we arrive to our next result.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
r\in[1,\infty)
and
\mu\in{\mathscr{P}_{r}}(\mathbb{X})
, then there exists a sequence
\{f^{\mu}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C(\mathbb{X})
such that
|f^{\mu}_{k}(x)|\leq C(f^{\mu}_{k})(1+\rho(x_{0},x)^{r})
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that a sequence of probability measures
\{\mu_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
converges weakly to
\mu
if and only if

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{X}}f^{\mu}_{k}(x)\,\mu_{m}(\mathrm{d}x)\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{}\int_{\mathbb{X}}f^{\mu}_{k}(x)\,\mu(\mathrm{d}x),\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N}.

Proof.

For every open ball with center
\mu
and radius
\frac{1}{m}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, denoted by
B_{W_{r,\rho}}(\mu,\frac{1}{m})
, there exists
D_{m}\in\textbf{B}_{r}(\mathcal{T}_{r})
where
\mu\in D_{m}\subseteq B_{W_{r,\rho}}(\mu,\frac{1}{m})
.
Using (
2.29
), we can conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 2.13
.

One can view the Wasserstein distance of order
r
as a non-negative function on the Polish space
(\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X}),\mathcal{T})\times(\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X}),\mathcal{T})
, with the possibility of taking infinite values.
Then, from
[
60
, Remark 6.12]
we have that
\mathcal{W}_{r,\rho}
is lower semi-continuous, hence measurable.

A useful inequality for the Wasserstein distance of order
r
, which is going to be used multiple times hereinafter, concerns the distance between two empirical measures on
\mathbb{X}
.
Given
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{x}^{N}:=(x^{1},\dots,x^{N}),

\mathbf{y}^{N}:=(y^{1},\dots,y^{N})\in\mathbb{X}^{N}
, we have the empirical measures

\displaystyle L^{N}(\mathbf{x}^{N}):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x^{i}}\text{ and }L^{N}(\mathbf{y}^{N}):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{y^{i}},

where
\delta_{x}
is the Dirac measure on
x\in\mathbb{X}
.
Then, we have

(2.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{r,\rho}^{r}\big(L^{N}(\mathbf{x}^{N}),L^{N}(\mathbf{y}^{N})\big)\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\rho(x^{i},y^{i})^{r}.

The above inequality is immediate if in the definition of the Wasserstein distance (
2.30
) we choose the probability measure

\displaystyle\pi:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{(x^{i},y^{i})},

where
\delta_{(x,y)}
is the Dirac measure on
(x,y)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{X}
.
The marginals are immediately seen to be
L^{N}(\mathbf{x}^{N})
and
L^{N}(\mathbf{y}^{N})
.

Remark 2.14
.

Finally, note that if
\rho
is a bounded metric, then for every
r\in[1,\infty)
we have
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X})=\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathbb{X})
, and every function
f
as in (
2.28
) belongs to
C_{b}(\mathbb{X})
.
Hence, we see that
\mathcal{W}_{r,\rho}
metrizes the weak convergence on
\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X})
, see
[
60
, Corollary 6.13]
.

2.10.
Skorokhod space

Let us denote by
\mathbb{D}^{d}:=\{f:[0,\infty){}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}:f\text{ is c\`{a}dl\`{a}g}\}
the space of càdlàg paths for every
d\in\mathbb{N}
.
We supply
\mathbb{D}^{d}
with its usual
J_{1}
-metric, which we denote by
\rho_{J_{1}}
.
Endowed with this metric,
\mathbb{D}^{d}
becomes a Polish space.
We are not going to get into the specifics of
\rho_{J_{1}}
, as we will only need a couple of its basic properties.
Firstly, for every
x,y\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
, we have

(2.32)

\displaystyle\rho_{J_{1}}(x,y)\leq\sup_{s\in[0,\infty)}\{|x_{s}-y_{s}|\}\wedge 1.

Secondly, the Borel
\sigma-
algebra that
\rho_{J_{1}}
generates coincides with the usual product
\sigma-
algebra on
{(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{[0,\infty)}
that the projections generate.
To be more precise, we have

(2.33)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d})=\sigma\Big(\textrm{Proj}_{s}^{-1}(A):A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),s\in[0,\infty)\Big)\cap\mathbb{D}^{d},

where
{(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{[0,\infty)}\ni x\overset{\textrm{Proj}_{s}}{\longmapsto}x(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for every
s\in[0,\infty)
.
Additional results on the Skorokhod space are available from
[
32
, Chapter 15]
or
[
39
, Chapter VI]
.

Using the fact that the
\sigma-
algebra on
\Omega
is
\mathcal{G}
, it is obvious from (
2.33
) that every
\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))-
jointly measurable càdlàg process
X
can be seen as a function with domain
\Omega
and taking values in
\mathbb{D}^{d}
such that
X
is
\big(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d})\big)-
measurable and
vice versa
: every
\big(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d})\big)-
measurable random variable
X
can be seen as a
\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{B}([0,\infty))-
jointly measurable càdlàg process.

Remark 2.15
.

Later, when we say that a collection of càdlàg processes is independent or is identically distributed or is exchangeable, they will be understood as
\big(\mathcal{G}/\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d})\big)-
measurable random variables.

Finally, for every
a\in(0,\infty)
and
x\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
, the initial segment
x|_{[0,a]}
, resp.
x|_{[0,a-]}
, will be understood as an element of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
, using the convention

(2.34)

\displaystyle x|_{[0,a]}(s):=x(s)\mathds{1}_{[0,a)}(s)+x(a)\mathds{1}_{[a,\infty)}(s),

\displaystyle\text{ resp. }x|_{[0,a-]}(s):=x(s)\mathds{1}_{[0,a)}(s)+x(a-)\mathds{1}_{[a,\infty)}(s).

For
a\in[0,\infty)
, we define
\mathbb{D}^{d}_{a}:=\{x_{[0,a]}:x\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\}
, resp.
\mathbb{D}^{d}_{a-}:=\{x_{[0,a-]}:x\in\mathbb{D}^{d}\}
, and we naturally have

\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d}_{a})=\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d})\cap\mathbb{D}^{d}_{a},\quad\text{ resp. }\quad\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d}_{a-})=\mathcal{B}_{\rho_{J_{1}}}(\mathbb{D}^{d})\cap\mathbb{D}^{d}_{a-}.

3.
A priori estimates

In this section, we provide
a priori
estimates for BSDEs whose generator does not depend on the solution of the equation.
In other words, we deal with semimartingale representations, for which their norms, as well as the norm of their martingale parts, can be controlled in terms of the norms of the terminal value and of the integrated generator of the equation.
These estimates will be our primary tool in order to prove the existence and uniqueness theorems for the mean-field as well as the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs in the subsequent sections, but also in order to prove the propagation of chaos statements.
They expand the results of
Papapantoleon et al. [53, Section 3.4]
by replacing the deterministic exponential with the stochastic one.
Two remarks are in order at this point:
Firstly, the use of the left-continuous stochastic exponential as the norm-weight allows to obtain a statement analogous to
[
53
, Lemma 3.8]
.
Secondly, similar
a priori
estimates for BSDEs have recently been obtained independently by
Possamaï and Rodrigues [55]
.

Intuitively speaking, the last term in (
3.1
) below is zero on average, as martingales are the stochastic analogs of deterministic constant functions.
Because the norms are defined using an expectation, it should be possible to bound the norm of the solution
y
using the norms of the generator
f
and the terminal condition
\xi
.
Then, it follows directly that one can bound the norm of
\eta
using the norms of
f
and
\xi
, by switching places in (
3.1
).

Recall that
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P})
denotes a complete stochastic basis, which we assume fixed within this section.
In addition, fix a stopping time
T
, a predictable, càdlàg, non-decreasing process
C
, a predictable, non-vanishing, real-valued process
\alpha
and define
A_{\cdot}:=\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha_{s}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}
.
In an effort to ease the notation within the proof of
Lemma

3.1
, we will omit the dependence of the norms on
\mathbb{G}
,
\mathcal{G}_{T}
,
C
,
A
and the state space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
More precisely,
\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
will be simply denoted by

\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\beta}
,

\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}
,

\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}
and

\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}
respectively.

Lemma 3.1
.

Assume that we are given a
d
-dimensional semimartingale
y
of the form

(3.1)

y_{t}=\xi+\int_{t}^{T}f_{s}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}-\int_{t}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\eta_{s},

where
\xi\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

f
is a
d
-dimensional optional process, and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
In addition, assume that there exists some
\Phi\geq 0
such that
\Delta A\leq\Phi,\mathbb{P}\otimes C-
almost everywhere.
Moreover, suppose that there exists a
\beta\in(0,\infty)
such that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\|\xi\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty.

Then, for any
(\gamma,\delta)\in(0,\beta]^{2}
with
\gamma\neq\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\|\alpha y\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{2(1+\delta\Phi)}{\delta}\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+2\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 8\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\frac{8(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
\leq(18+9\delta\Phi)\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\Big(\frac{9{\big(1+(\gamma\vee\delta)\Phi\big)}}{\gamma\vee\delta}+\frac{9\delta(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\Big)\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}.\end{multlined}

Therefore, putting the pieces together, we have

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
\leq(26+9\delta\Phi)\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
+\Big(\frac{8}{\gamma}+17\Phi+\frac{9}{\gamma\vee\delta}+9\delta\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\Big)\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\end{multlined}

and

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\|\alpha y\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
\leq\Big(26+\frac{2}{\delta}+(9\delta+2)\Phi\Big)\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\\
+\Big(\frac{8}{\gamma}+17\Phi+\frac{9}{\gamma\vee\delta}+(9\delta+2)\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\Big)\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\end{multlined}.

Proof.

We will initially deal with the upper bounds of the norms of the semimartingale
y
.
By definition, we have that
\int_{t}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\eta_{s}=\eta_{T}-\eta_{T\wedge t}
.
Because
y
is adapted and
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have, for every
t\geq 0
, that

(3.3)

y_{t}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\xi+\int_{t}^{T}f_{s}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\bigg].

Hence, from the above identity it is evident that we need to study the following process

(3.4)

\displaystyle F(t):=\int_{t}^{T}f_{s}\mathrm{d}C_{s}.

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, then we have from the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality and
Lemma

2.4
.
(viii)
that

\displaystyle|F(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{s-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{s}\int_{t}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{t-}\int_{t}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}.

Using the assumption in (
3.2
), for
\gamma\in(0,\beta]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|F(0)|^{2}\right]<\infty.

Next, for
\delta\in(0,\beta]
, we will integrate
|F(t)|^{2}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{-}\mathrm{d}A
,
i.e.
, with respect to
\delta^{-1}\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)
.
Before we proceed, we will use the notation of
Lemma

2.4
.
(vii)
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}_{\cdot}:=\delta A_{\cdot}-\overline{\gamma A}_{\cdot}-[\delta A,\overline{\gamma A}]_{\cdot},

for
\overline{\gamma A}
defined as in
Lemma

2.4
.
(ii)
.
We underline that
\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})
is (strictly) positive.
Indeed, this is straightforward from
(iii)
and
(vii)
of
Lemma

2.4
.
Now, we return to our aim and, with the help of
Lemma

2.4
.
(vii)
, and Tonelli’s theorem, we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|F(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{aprioriineq1}}{\leq}\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\mathds{1}_{\rrbracket t,T\rrbracket}(s)\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{\eqref{aprioriineq1}}}{=}\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\mathds{1}_{\rrbracket t,T\rrbracket}(s)\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}

(3.6)

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{\eqref{aprioriineq1}}}{=}\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\int_{0}^{s-}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}.

Let us concentrate for a moment on the term

\int_{0}^{s-}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\mathrm{d}A_{t}
, by considering the two cases
\delta>\gamma
and
\delta<\gamma
:

\bullet\,\delta>\gamma
:
Using
Lemma

2.4
.
(vii)
we derive the inequality

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s-}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta-\gamma}\int_{0}^{s-}(1+\Delta(\gamma A)_{t})\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}_{t}

(3.7)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\delta-\gamma}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{s-}.

Thus, returning to (
3.6
) and in conjunction with (
3.7
) and
Lemma

2.4
.
(vii)
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|F(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}=\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma(\delta-\gamma)}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s},

which is integrable for
\delta\leq\beta
.

\bullet\hskip 2.84544pt\delta<\gamma
:
Using
Lemma

2.4
.
(vii)
again, we deduce

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s-}\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{\eqref{equation:stochexp}}}{=}\frac{1}{\delta-\gamma}\int_{0}^{s-}(1+\Delta(\gamma A)_{t})\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{t-}\,\mathrm{d}\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma}_{t}

(3.8)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{equation:stochexp}}{=}\frac{1}{|\delta-\gamma|}\int_{0}^{s-}(1+\Delta(\gamma A)_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\big(-\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})\big)_{t}\leq\frac{1+\gamma\Phi}{|\delta-\gamma|},

where, for the inequality, we use that
\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})
is positive and non-decreasing; see
(vii)
and
(iii)
of
Lemma

2.4
. Thus, returning to (
3.6
) and in conjunction with (
3.8
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|F(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:case_gamma_greater_delta_1}}{\leq}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}.

In total, summing up the conclusions of the two cases, we have that for every
(\gamma,\delta)\in(0,\beta]^{2}
with
\gamma\neq\delta
we can rewrite (
3.6
) as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|F(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\leq\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}\big((\gamma\vee\delta)A\big)_{s-}\frac{|f_{s}|^{2}}{\alpha^{2}_{s}}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}

or, equivalently – in terms of the introduced notation – as

(3.9)

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|F(t)|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\bigg]\leq\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}}.

We are now ready to estimate
\|\alpha y\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}}
. Using (
3.3
), (
3.4
), Jensen’s inequality, the inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
, in conjunction with the fact that
A
is predictable, see
[
32
, Theorem 5.13]
, and (
3.9
), we have that

\displaystyle\|\alpha y\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|y_{t}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\bigg]\leq\frac{2(1+\delta\Phi)}{\delta}\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}}+2\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}}.

We move on to the estimate of
\|y\|_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}}
.
Once again, we will use (
3.3
), (
3.4
), (
3.5
), the vector analogue of the triangle inequality for conditional expectations, the inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
and the subadditivity of the square root function,
i.e.
,

\displaystyle\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\left(\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|y_{t}|^{2}\right)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big(\mathcal{E}(\delta A)^{\frac{1}{2}}_{t-}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\xi+F(t)\big|\Big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\Big]\Big)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|\xi|^{2}+\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|F(t)|^{2}}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\Big]\Big)^{2}\Big].

At this point, we will split our analysis again in two cases:

\bullet\hskip 2.84544pt\delta<\gamma
: By definition of the stochastic exponential, for
A
increasing we have
0<\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\leq\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{t-}
or equivalently
0<\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{t-}\leq 1
.
Hence, by means of (
3.5
) and Doob’s inequality we get

\displaystyle\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\leq 8\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}}+\frac{8(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma}}.

\bullet\,\delta>\gamma
:
We will use the fact that
\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{\cdot}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{\cdot}=\mathcal{E}(\widetilde{A}^{\delta,\gamma})_{\cdot}
is non-decreasing and positive; see in
Lemma

2.4

(iv)
and
(vii)
, in conjunction with
(iii)
. Now, we can proceed exactly as in the previous case except that in the current case we transfer the term

\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{t-}
inside the integral and we bound it from above by
\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{s-}\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)^{-1}_{s-}
, for
s\geq t
.
After cancellation, we get the same formulas with the difference that inside the Lebesgue–Stieltjes integral we have
\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{s-}
in place of
\mathcal{E}(\gamma A)_{s-}
.

Combining the two cases, we get

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}}\leq 8\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}}+\frac{8(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}}.

What remains to derive the bound for
\|\eta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\delta}}
. Let
\eta=(\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{d})
. We have per coordinate
i\in\{1,\dots,d\}
that

\displaystyle(\eta^{i}_{T}-\eta^{i}_{T\wedge t})^{2}

\displaystyle=(\eta^{i}_{T})^{2}-(\eta^{i}_{T\wedge t})^{2}-2\eta^{i}_{T\wedge t}(\eta^{i}_{T}-\eta^{i}_{T\wedge t})

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}=\big((\eta^{i}_{T})^{2}-\langle\eta^{i}\rangle_{T}\big)-\big((\eta^{i}_{T\wedge t})^{2}-\langle\eta^{i}\rangle_{T\wedge t}\big)\\
-2\eta^{i}_{T\wedge t}(\eta^{i}_{T}-\eta^{i}_{T\wedge t})+\langle\eta^{i}\rangle_{T}-\langle\eta^{i}\rangle_{T\wedge t}.\end{multlined}

Hence, because for every
\mathbb{G}-
martingale
M
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
holds that
\mathbb{E}\left[M_{T}\big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\right]=M_{T\wedge t}
and
\eta^{i},(\eta^{i})^{2}-\langle\eta^{i}\rangle
are
\mathbb{G}-
martingales, we have from the linearity of the conditional expectation that

(3.11)

\mathbb{E}[|\xi-y_{t}+F(t)|^{2}|\mathcal{G}_{t}]\overset{\eqref{apriorieq}}{=}\mathbb{E}[|\eta_{T}-\eta_{T\wedge t}|^{2}|\mathcal{G}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]\Big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\bigg].

Direct computations via Tonelli’s theorem yield

\displaystyle\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\delta}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{equation:stochexp}}{=}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\delta\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{s-}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\,\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]+\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]\bigg]

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{\eqref{equation:stochexp}}}{=}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\delta\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\int_{t}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}+\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]\bigg].

Regarding the first term on the right side of the above inequality, using the fact that
A
is predictable, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\int_{t}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]\Big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\bigg]\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\overset{\eqref{3.12}}{=}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\mathbb{E}\big[|\xi-y_{t}+F(t)|^{2}\big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\big]\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\overset{\eqref{apriorieq2}}{\underset{\phantom{\eqref{aprioriineq3}}}{\leq}}9\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}|\xi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\bigg]+9\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\delta A)_{t-}\mathbb{E}\big[|F(t)|^{2}\big|\mathcal{G}_{t}\big]\,\mathrm{d}A_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\overset{\eqref{aprioriineq3}}{\leq}\frac{9(1+\delta\Phi)}{\delta}\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}}+\frac{9(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}}.

Regarding the second term, we have for every
\epsilon,\zeta\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{d}\text{Tr}[\langle\eta\rangle_{s}]\bigg]

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{3.12}}{\underset{\eqref{apriorieq2}}{\leq}}9\mathbb{E}\big[|\xi|^{2}\big]+9\mathbb{E}\big[|F(0)|^{2}\big]\overset{\eqref{aprioriineq1}}{\leq}9\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\epsilon}}+\frac{9{\big(1+\zeta\Phi\big)}}{\zeta}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\zeta}}.

Combining all of the above, we finally arrive at the following

\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\delta}}\leq(18+9\delta\Phi)\hskip 2.84544pt\|\xi\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\delta}}+\Big(\frac{9{\big(1+(\gamma\vee\delta)\Phi\big)}}{\gamma\vee\delta}+\frac{9\delta(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma|\delta-\gamma|}\Big)\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\gamma\vee\delta}}.\qed

Corollary 3.2
.

Assume the framework of
Lemma

3.1
with
\xi\equiv 0
,
\beta\in(0,\infty)
and
0\leq\gamma<\delta\leq\beta
.
Then,

\displaystyle\|\alpha y\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma(\delta-\gamma)}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})},

\displaystyle\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\frac{4(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}

and

\displaystyle\|\eta\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\Big(\frac{4{\big(1+\delta\Phi\big)}}{\delta}+\frac{4\delta(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma(\delta-\gamma)}\Big)\left\|\frac{f}{\alpha}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\delta}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The previous lemma, as well as its
Corollary

3.2
, will be crucial in the construction of the contraction operators of the main theorems dealing with the existence and uniqueness of solutions of mean-field and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs.
Foreseeing the coefficients that appear in the aforementioned procedure, we define them here and provide the respective optimality results in the following Proposition.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}:=\{(\gamma,\delta)\in(0,\beta]^{2}:\gamma<\delta\}
and define

\displaystyle M_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta):=\inf_{(\gamma,\delta)\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta}}\Big\{\frac{9(1+\delta\Phi)}{\delta}+\frac{8(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{9\delta}{\delta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\Big\}

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta):=\inf_{(\gamma,\delta)\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta}}\Big\{\frac{4(1+\delta\Phi)}{\delta}+\frac{4(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{4\delta}{\delta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\Big\}

\displaystyle M_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta):=\inf_{(\gamma,\delta)\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta}}\Big\{\frac{9(1+\delta\Phi)}{\delta}+\frac{8(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{9\delta+2}{\delta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\Big\}

and

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta):=\inf_{(\gamma,\delta)\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta}}\Big\{\frac{4(1+\delta\Phi)}{\delta}+\frac{4(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{4\delta+1}{\delta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\Big\}.

We have the following obvious comparisons

(3.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{M}_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta)<M_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta)\text{ and }\widehat{M}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta)<M_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta).

In order to complete our analysis, we provide below asymptotic bounds for
M_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta)
and
M_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta)
with respect to
\Phi
.

A few remarks are appropriate at this point, in order to explain their roles and clarify the choices we made.
Let us start with an explanation of the notation.
Obviously, the lower index indicates the norm with which the coefficient are associated.
The presence of the wide hat indicates that the coefficients were obtained through
Corollary

3.2
, while the absence of the wide hat indicates that the coefficients were obtained through
Lemma

3.1
.

Let us focus now on their application.
In the literature on BSDEs, the application of
Corollary

3.2
is the appropriate way to construct the optimal contraction constant for the existence and uniqueness results.
This is indeed the case because the processes mapped via the contraction have – by construction – the same terminal random variable.
Hence, the respective difference vanishes yielding
Corollary

3.2
the optimal option at this point.
However, in order to apply the
a priori
estimates in the proof of the backward propagation of chaos, we need to control differences between the mean-field system and the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs, where the terminal conditions will not vanish.
This generality dictates the use of
Lemma

3.1
.

Remark 3.3
.

Let us point out that the discrepancy of the coefficients between
Lemma

3.1
and
Proposition

3.4
in this work, and
Possamaï and Rodrigues [55, Proposition 5.4.]
is due to the difference in the definition of the norms, where we use the left limit of the stochastic exponential instead of the right one as in
[
55
]
, while they also focus on the special case where
\xi=0
(when adapted to our notation).

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\Phi\geq 0
and
\beta\in(0,\infty)
, then we have

(3.13)

\displaystyle M_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\min_{\gamma\in(0,\beta)}\left\{\frac{9}{\beta}+9\Phi+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+9\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(6\sqrt{17}+35\right)\left(\frac{1}{\beta}+\Phi\right)

and

(3.14)

\displaystyle M_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\min_{\gamma\in(0,\beta)}\left\{\frac{9}{\beta}+9\Phi+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{2+9\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(2\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}+9}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}+17}+\frac{4}{\beta}+35\right)\left(\frac{1}{\beta}+\Phi\right).

Hence, we get that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\infty}M_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta)=\lim_{\beta\rightarrow\infty}M_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta)=\left(6\sqrt{17}+35\right)\Phi.

Proof.

We will present here only the part of the results that is needed in the main text,
i.e.
for (
3.13
) and (
3.14
).
The remainder of this proof is omitted as it also involves only elementary calculus, but is available upon request.

Using the definition of
M_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta),M_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta)
we can make a couple of observations.
The first one is that we should only examine the case
\delta=\beta
, because for every pair
(\gamma,\delta)\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{9}{\delta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+9\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{\delta}{\delta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{9}{\beta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+9\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma},

\displaystyle\frac{9}{\delta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{2+9\delta}{\delta-\gamma}\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{9}{\beta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{2+9\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}.

The second one is that

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{9}{\beta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+9\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow\beta^{-}}\frac{9}{\beta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+9\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}

\displaystyle=+\infty

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{9}{\beta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{2+9\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow\beta^{-}}\frac{9}{\beta}+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{2+9\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}

\displaystyle=+\infty.

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle M_{\star}^{\Phi}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\min_{\gamma\in(0,\beta)}\left\{\frac{9}{\beta}+9\Phi+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+9\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\right\}

and

\displaystyle M_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}^{\Phi}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\min_{\gamma\in(0,\beta)}\left\{\frac{9}{\beta}+9\Phi+8\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)}{\gamma}+\frac{2+9\beta}{\beta-\gamma}\hskip 2.84544pt\frac{(1+\gamma\Phi)^{2}}{\gamma}\right\}.

∎

4.
Well-posedness of mean-field and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs

In this section, we provide general existence and uniqueness results for mean-field systems of BSDEs and for McKean–Vlasov BSDEs, in a setting where the filtrations are stochastically discontinuous and the stochastic integrals are defined with respect to general
\mathbb{L}^{2}
–martingales.
In other words, we consider discrete-time and continuous-time mean-field systems of BSDEs and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs in a unified setting, where the driving processes are general and include both diffusions and jumps.
We consider first a “path-dependent” version of mean-field systems of BSDEs and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs, where the generator depends on the initial segment of the solution
Y
, see
(

MF
Seg

¯
S

mod

) and
(

MV
Seg

Y

).
Then, we also provide existence and uniqueness results for “classic” mean-field systems of BSDEs and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs that depend only on the instantaneous value of
Y
, see (
1
) and (
1
), under weaker assumptions.

4.1.
Mean-field system of BSDEs

4.1.1.
Framework and structure of mean-field systems of BSDEs

We will first introduce the setting for the initial existence and uniqueness theorem, which concerns mean-field systems of BSDEs,
i.e.
, systems of BSDEs where the empirical measure of the processes affects the generator of the equation.
Let us fix a stochastic basis
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P})
, a
\mathbb{G}-
stopping time
T
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
and a
\hat{\beta}>0
; recall that
\mathscr{N}=\{1,\dots,N\}
.
The stochastic basis supports the following:

(MF1)

The family of martingales
(\mathbf{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
, where for each
i\in\mathscr{N}

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}^{i}=(X^{i,\circ},X^{i,\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that
M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}[\Delta X^{i,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0.
7
7

7

Since the filtration
\mathbb{G}
is given as well as the pairs
\mathbf{X}^{i}

Moreover, we will use the kernels

(MF2)

The generator

, where for

\displaystyle f^{i,N}:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{X})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d},

where

and

9
9

In the current work,

For the sake of simplicity, we introduce the condition

\mathbb{X}
and

The generator is, additionally, such that for any
(y,z,u,\mu)

\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{X})
the map

\displaystyle(\omega,t)\longmapsto f^{N}(\omega,t,y,z,u,\mu)\text{ is }\mathbb{G}-\text{progressively measurable}

and satisfies the following (stochastic) Lipschitz condition: for any
(y,z,u,\mu)
,
(y^{\prime},z^{\prime},u^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{X})
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}|f^{i,N}(\omega,t,y,z,u,\mu)-f^{i,N}(\omega,t,y^{\prime},z^{\prime},u^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})|^{2}\\
\leq r^{i,N}(\omega,t)\rho_{\mathbb{X}}(y,y^{\prime})^{2}+\vartheta^{i,N,\circ}(\omega,t)|z-z^{\prime}|^{2}\\
+\vartheta^{i,N,\natural}(\omega,t)|u-u^{\prime}|^{2}+\vartheta^{i,N,*}(\omega,t)\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\left(\mu,\mu^{\prime}\right),\end{multlined}

where
(r,\vartheta^{\circ},\vartheta^{\natural},\vartheta^{i,N,*}):\left(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{+},\mathcal{B}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{+}\right)\right)
.

(MF3)

The
\mathbb{G}
–predictable and càdlàg processes

\displaystyle A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},f^{N})}_{\cdot}:=\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{2}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}_{s},

are such that
there exists
\Phi>0
with

\displaystyle\Delta A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},f^{N})}_{t}(\omega)\leq\Phi,\ \mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}-\text{a.e.},

for all

,
where
\alpha^{2}_{\cdot}:=\max\{(\sqrt{r^{i}_{\cdot}})_{i\in\mathscr{N}},(\vartheta^{i,N,\circ}_{\cdot})_{i\in\mathscr{N}},(\vartheta^{i,N,\natural}_{\cdot})_{i\in\mathscr{N}},\big(h(\vartheta^{i,N,*}_{\cdot})\big)_{i\in\mathscr{N}}\}
, with
\alpha^{2}>0
, for a function

to be specified.
10
10

10

We suppress the dependence on the choice of the function

as in the statement of each theorem this function will be fixed.

(MF4)

(i)

There exists

\displaystyle\max_{i\in\mathscr{N}}\Big\{\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},f^{N})}\big)_{T}\Big\}\leq\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}.

(ii)

For

11

(MF5)

Remark 4.1
.

(i)

In the description of
(MF3)
, more precisely in the definition of
\alpha^{2}
, the introduction of the function
h
appears to be unavoidable in order to maintain a simple and unified presentation of the framework.
Let us initially focus on the other terms that appear in the definition of
\alpha^{2}
.
That is, the bounds
r^{i,N},\vartheta^{i,N,\circ}
and
\vartheta^{i,N,\natural}
.
In view of the definition of
\alpha^{2}
, see
(MF3)
, we have for
i\in\mathscr{N}
and
(\omega,t)\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\frac{r^{i,N}_{t}}{\alpha^{2}_{t}}\leq\alpha^{2}_{t},\quad\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,\circ}_{t}}{\alpha^{2}_{t}}\leq 1\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,\natural}_{t}}{\alpha^{2}_{t}}\leq 1.

In the course of the computations, a similar bound will be needed for
\vartheta^{i,N,*}
.
However, the structure of
\mathscr{X}
dictates different approaches.
On the one hand, when
\mathscr{X}
is the product space
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
the function
h
is specified as the identity.
This leads to the bound

(4.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,*}_{t}}{\alpha^{2}_{t}}\leq 1,

for
i\in\mathscr{N}
and
(\omega,t)\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
.
In other words, the bounds
(\vartheta^{i,N,*})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
should behave as the bounds
(\vartheta^{i,N,\circ})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
and
(\vartheta^{i,N,\natural})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
.
Without delving into further details at this point, this bound should be complemented with the additional condition
\alpha^{2}\geq 1
in order for the desired norms to be constructed; the details are presented in the proofs of
Theorems

4.2
and
4.4
.
On the other hand, when
\mathscr{X}
is the space
\mathbb{X}
the function
h
is specified as the square root.
This leads to the bound

(4.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,*}_{t}}{\alpha^{2}_{t}}\leq\alpha^{2}_{t},

for
i\in\mathscr{N}
and
(\omega,t)\in\llbracket 0,T\rrbracket
.
In other words, the bounds
(\vartheta^{i,N,*})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
should behave as the bounds
(r^{i})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
; the details are presented in the proofs of
Theorems

4.3
and
4.5
.

(ii)

Assumption
(MF4)
.
(MF4)(ii)
is not restrictive.
Indeed, this is the case when

(\mathbf{X}^{i})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
is a family whose elements are identically distributed and define deterministic processes
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}
, i.e., from
Proposition

5.2
have independent increments.
Then,
from (
2.9
) and
He et al. [32, 6.23 Theorem]
we would have for
i,j\in\mathscr{N}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
that

\displaystyle C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}_{t}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\textup{Tr}\big[\langle X^{i,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}\big]\Big]+\mathbb{E}\left[|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{i,\natural})}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{i,\circ}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\left[|I|^{2}*\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{i,\circ}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{i,\natural}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{j,\circ}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{j,\natural}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{j,\circ}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]+\mathbb{E}\left[|I|^{2}*\mu^{X^{j,\natural}}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\textup{Tr}\big[\langle X^{j,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}\big]\Big]+\mathbb{E}\left[|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{j,\natural})}_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\Big[C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{j})}_{t}\Big]=C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{j})}_{t}.

In fact, the above argument includes that

\textup{Tr}\big[\langle X^{i,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}]=\textup{Tr}\big[\langle X^{j,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}]\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046pt|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{i,\natural})}_{t}=|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{j,\natural})}_{t}.

More generally, in this situation, we have

\displaystyle\langle X^{i,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle X^{i,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[X^{i,\circ}_{t}\cdot\left(X^{i,\circ}\right)^{\top}_{t}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[X^{j,\circ}_{t}\cdot\left(X^{j,\circ}\right)^{\top}_{t}\Big]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\langle X^{j,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}\Big]=\langle X^{j,\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}_{t}.

(iii)

Let us repeat that Assumption
(MF4)
.
(MF4)(i)
(and
(MV4)

(MV4)(i)
below) leads to equivalence between the
\hat{\beta}
–norms and their

However, we will need the
\hat{\beta}-

Now, let us describe the structure of the mean-field we will consider.
As we have already indicated in
(MF2)
, the metric space
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})
will be either
(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
or

or

This generality allows us to describe the problem of solving a mean-field BSDE system as seeking for a process
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}:=(\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}

\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}:=(Y^{i,N},Z^{i,N},U^{i,N},M^{i,N})

12

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}\in\mathcal{S}^{2}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},f^{N})};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},f^{N})},X^{i,\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})\\

(MF)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y^{i,N}_{t}=\xi^{i,N}+\int^{T}_{t}f^{i,N}\Big(s,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N},L^{N}\big(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z^{i,N}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i,\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U^{i,N}_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{i,\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M^{i,N}_{s},\end{multlined}

for

and

.
13
13

The modification is not needed when

; see

4.3

Before we proceed, let us emphasize once again that the aforementioned BSDE is indeed a system due to the dependence of the generator
f^{i,N}
on the empirical measure of
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}
.
Had the empirical measure not been there, we would simply have

Let us return to the two main cases, depending on the choice of the metric space

On the one hand, when

In the latter case, the mean-field BSDE system (

(

of the solution process but the empirical measure only on the

On the other hand, when

)

-\int^{T}_{t}Z^{i,N}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i,\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U^{i,N}_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{i,\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M^{i,N}_{s}\end{multlined}

for

, with

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{\cdot}:=(Y^{1,N}_{\cdot},\dots,Y^{N,N}_{\cdot}).

in other words, the system depends on the instantaneous value of the solution process but the empirical measure only on the

-component of the solution process.

4.1.2.
Statements of the mean-field BSDE theorems

Let us now state the main theorems on the existence and uniqueness of the solution for the aforementioned mean-field BSDE systems.

Theorem 4.2
.

Under
(MF1)
,
(MF2)
for
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
and
(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p},\rho_{\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}})
,
(MF3)
for
[0,\infty)\ni x\overset{h}{\mapsto}x
and
\alpha^{2}_{\cdot}\geq 1
,
(MF4)
,
(MF5)
,
(MF6)
and
(MF7)
, if

Remark 4.7
.

The last two statements differ only in terms of the conditions and norms of the solution.

Remark 4.8
.

We have avoided using product spaces in the statement of the aforementioned theorems for the sake of notational simplicity.
However, the use of product spaces will be handy within the proofs.
To this end, let us mention once again that we adopt the following convention hereinafter: whenever we consider a (finite) Cartesian product of normed spaces, the squared norm on this product will be the sum of the squared norms of the normed spaces used to construct the Cartesian product, and it will be simply denoted by
\|\cdot\|
, except if otherwise stated.

4.1.3.
Proofs of the mean-field BSDE theorems

Given that we have fixed our framework and the associated data, and for the sake of notational simplicity (again) within the proofs, we will drop the (seemingly) “redundant” notation as follows: for
i\in\mathscr{N}

the objects
A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},f^{N})}
,
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}
,
c{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}
,

K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}
,

\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},\Theta^{i})}
,
and
\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}

will be denoted by
A^{i}
,
C^{i}
,
c^{i}
,
K^{i}
,

\Gamma^{i}
, and
\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{i}
respectively.
Moreover, within the proofs, we will omit the dependence of the norms on
\mathbb{G}
,
\mathcal{G}_{T}
,
C^{i}
,
A^{i}
and the Euclidean state spaces.
More precisely, for
\beta\geq 0
, the spaces

\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A^{i};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A^{i};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathscr{S}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A^{i};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A^{i},C^{i};\mathbb{R}^{d})

\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A^{i},X^{i,\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})
,

\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A^{i},\mu^{X^{i,\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A^{i},(\mathbf{X}^{i})^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
and

\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\beta}(\mathbb{G},A^{i};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
will be simply denoted by

\mathbb{L}^{2,i}_{\beta}
,

\mathcal{S}^{2,i}_{\beta}
,

\mathscr{S}^{2,i}_{\beta}
,

\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\beta}
,

\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\circ}_{\beta}
,

\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\natural}_{\beta}
,

\mathcal{H}^{2,i,\perp}_{\beta}

and

\mathcal{H}^{2,i}_{\beta}

respectively.
Additionally, for
\beta\geq 0
, we introduce the following notation for product spaces:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\beta,N}:=\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{S}^{2,i}_{\beta},\hskip 5.0pt\mathscr{S}^{2}_{\beta,N}:=\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathscr{S}^{2,i}_{\beta},\hskip 5.0pt\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\beta,N}:=\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\beta},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}^{2,\circ}_{\beta,N}:=\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\circ}_{\beta},\hskip 5.0pt\mathbb{H}^{2,\natural}_{\beta,N}:=\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\natural}_{\beta},\hskip 5.0pt\mathcal{H}^{2,\perp}_{\beta,N}:=\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{H}^{2,i,\perp}_{\beta}

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\beta,N}:=\mathbb{H}^{2,\circ}_{\beta,N}\times\mathbb{H}^{2,\natural}_{\beta,N}\times\mathcal{H}^{2,\perp}_{\beta,N}.

We will present in sufficient detail the proof of
Theorem

4.2
, as this will serve as the reference proof for the other existence and uniqueness results.

Proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

We will follow the classical approach for BSDEs defined at that generality, see
e.g.

El Karoui and Huang [28]
,
Papapantoleon et al. [53, Theorem 3.5]
or
Possamaï and Rodrigues [55]
.
Let

\displaystyle(\mathbf{y}^{N},\mathbf{z}^{N},\mathbf{u}^{N},\mathbf{m}^{N})\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}

with

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{N}:=(y^{1,N},\ldots,y^{N,N}),\mathbf{z}^{N}:=(z^{1,N},\ldots,z^{N,N}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{N}:=(u^{1,N},\ldots,u^{N,N})\text{ and }\mathbf{m}^{N}:=(m^{1,N},\ldots,m^{N,N}).

Let us also define

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{i,N}_{\cdot}:=(y^{i,N}|_{[0,\cdot]},z^{i,N}_{\cdot},u^{i,N}_{\cdot},m^{i,N}_{\cdot})

and

\displaystyle\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N}_{\cdot}:=\big(y^{i,N}|_{[0,\cdot]},z^{i,N}_{\cdot}c^{i}_{\cdot},\Gamma^{i}(u^{i,N})_{\cdot}\big)

for
i\in\mathscr{N}
.
Then, from
subsection

2.5
, in conjunction with
Lemma

3.1
(for
0<\gamma<\delta\equiv\hat{\beta}
), we have the existence of

\displaystyle({\mathbf{Y}}^{N},{\mathbf{Z}}^{N},{\mathbf{U}}^{N},{\mathbf{M}}^{N})\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}

with

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}^{N}:=(Y^{1,N},\ldots,Y^{N,N}),\mathbf{Z}^{N}:=(Z^{1,N},\ldots,Z^{N,N}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{U}^{N}:=(U^{1,N},\ldots,U^{N,N})\text{ and }\mathbf{M}^{N}:=(M^{1,N},\ldots,M^{N,N}).

such that for every
i\in\mathscr{N}
the following martingale

\displaystyle L^{i,N}_{\cdot}:=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\xi^{i,N}+\int_{0}^{T}f^{i,N}\big(s,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N}_{s},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\textrm{mod},1,N}_{s},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\textrm{mod},N,N}_{s})\big)\textup{d}C^{i}_{s}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{\cdot}\bigg]

is represented as

\displaystyle L^{i,N}_{\cdot}=\mathbb{E}\big[L_{T}^{i}|\mathcal{G}_{0}\big]+Z^{i}\cdot X^{i,\circ}_{\cdot}+U^{i}\star\widetilde{\mu}^{i,\natural}_{\cdot}+M^{i}_{\cdot}

and

Y^{i,N}_{t}:=\xi^{i,N}+\int^{T}_{t}f^{i,N}\left(s,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},i,N}_{s},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},1,N}_{s},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},N,N}_{s})\right)\,\mathrm{d}C^{i}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z^{i,N}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{i,\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U^{i,N}_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{i,\natural}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M^{i,N}_{s}.

Indeed, in view of
(MF6)
, the above claim is legitimate as long as the term corresponding to the second summand of Condition (
3.2
) is suitably integrable.
To this end, in view of
(MF2)
,
Lemma

2.9
, (
2.31
) and (
2.32
) we have the following for every
i\in\mathscr{N}
:

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f^{i,N}\left(\cdot,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},i,N}_{\cdot},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},1,N}_{\cdot},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},N,N}_{\cdot})\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq 2\left\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f^{i,N}\left(\cdot,0,0,0,\delta_{0}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}\\
\hskip-70.0001pt+2\big\|\frac{r^{i,N}}{\alpha}\sup_{(0,\cdot]}|y^{i,N}_{\cdot}|\big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\big\|\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,\circ}}{\alpha}z^{i,N}\big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+4\big\|\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,\natural}}{\alpha}u^{i,N}\big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\\
+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\bigg\{\big\|\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,*}}{\alpha}\sup_{(0,\cdot]}|y^{m,N}_{\cdot}|\big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}+\big\|\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,*}}{\alpha}z^{m,N}\big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\big\|\frac{\vartheta^{i,N,*}}{\alpha}u^{m,N}\big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\bigg\}\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\overset{\eqref{properties_stoch_bounds}}{\underset{\eqref{properties_stoch_bounds_onlyWasserstein_identity}}{\leq}}2\left\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f^{i,N}\left(\cdot,0,0,0,\delta_{0}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\frac{\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\|y^{i,N}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}}+2\|z^{i,N}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+4\|u^{i,N}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\\
+\frac{2}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\bigg\{\frac{\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\|y^{m,N}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}}+\|z^{m,N}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,m,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\|u^{m,N}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,m,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\bigg\}\end{multlined}

\displaystyle<\infty,

by
(MF7)
and our initial assumption on
(\mathbf{y}^{N},\mathbf{z}^{N},\mathbf{u}^{N},\mathbf{n}^{N})
.
We note that in the derivation of (
4.1.3
), more precisely in the construction of the stochastic exponentials whose bound
\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}
finally appears, we have used the assumption that
\alpha\geq 1
together with
(MF4)
(both
(MF4)(i)
and
(MF4)(ii)
).

Hence, we can indeed define the function

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{N}:\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\longrightarrow\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}

with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{N}(\mathbf{y}^{N},\mathbf{z}^{N},\mathbf{u}^{N},\mathbf{m}^{N}):=({\mathbf{Y}}^{N},{\mathbf{Z}}^{N},{\mathbf{U}}^{N},{\mathbf{M}}^{N}).

If
\mathfrak{S}^{N}
is a contraction then, using Banach’s fixed point theorem, we deduce that the solution is unique.
To this end, for
j=1,2
, let us arbitrarily choose

\displaystyle(\mathbf{y}^{N,(j)},\mathbf{z}^{N,(j)},\mathbf{u}^{N,(j)},\mathbf{m}^{N,(j)})\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}

with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}^{N}(\mathbf{y}^{N,(j)},\mathbf{z}^{N,(j)},\mathbf{u}^{N,(j)},\mathbf{m}^{N,(j)}):=({\mathbf{Y}}^{N,(j)},{\mathbf{Z}}^{N,(j)},{\mathbf{U}}^{N,(j)},{\mathbf{M}}^{N,(j)}),

with

\mathbf{y}^{N,(j)}:=(y^{1,N,(j)},\ldots,y^{N,N,(j)})
,
etc.
,

\mathbf{Y}^{N,(j)}:=(Y^{1,N,(j)},\ldots,Y^{N,N,(j)})
,
etc.

and
\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{i,N,(j)},\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N,(j)}
, for
i\in\mathscr{N}
, following the same rationale as above.
Additionally, for two similar objects indexed on
j
, say
\big(w^{(j)}\big)_{j=1,2}
, we will use the notation
\delta w^{(*)}:=w^{(2)}-w^{(1)}
;
e.g.
,
\delta\mathbf{y}^{N,(*)}=\mathbf{y}^{N,(2)}-\mathbf{y}^{N,(1)}
.
Following this rationale loosely, we define for every
i\in\mathscr{N}

(4.15)

\delta f^{i,N,(*)}:=f^{i,N}\left(s,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},i,N,(2)}_{s},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},1,N,(2)}_{s},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},N,N,(2)}_{s})\right)\\
-f^{i,N}\left(s,\overline{\mathbf{s}}_{s}^{\text{mod},i,N,(1)},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}_{s}^{\text{mod},1,N,(1)},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{s}}_{s}^{\text{mod},N,N,(1)})\right).

Following completely analogous arguments as in (
4.1.3
), we can extract the following inequality

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002pt\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}\delta f^{i,N,(*)}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big\{\frac{\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\|\delta y^{i,N,(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}+\|\delta z^{i,N,(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\|\delta u^{i,N,(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\Big\}\\
+\sum_{m=1}^{N}\bigg\{\frac{\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\|\delta y^{m,N,(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2,m}_{\hat{\beta}}}+\|\delta z^{m,N,(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,m,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\|\delta u^{m,N,(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,m,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\bigg\}\end{multlined}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq\max\Big\{4,\frac{2}{\hat{\beta}}\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}\Big\}\big\|\big(\delta\mathbf{y}^{N,(*)},\delta\mathbf{z}^{N,(*)},\delta\mathbf{u}^{N,(*)},\delta\mathbf{m}^{N,(*)}\big)\big\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}},N},\end{multlined}

for
i\in\mathscr{N}
.
Then, employing
Corollary

3.2
(
i.e.
, we use the fact that
\delta\mathbf{Y}^{N,(*)}_{T}=0
) per coordinate,
Proposition

3.4
and summing over
i\in\mathscr{N}
, we have (recall the notation for the norm on the Cartesian product)

\displaystyle\big\|\delta\mathfrak{S}^{N}(\mathbf{y}^{N,(*)},\mathbf{z}^{N,(*)},\mathbf{u}^{N,(*)},\mathbf{m}^{N,(*)})\big\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}}

\displaystyle\overset{\phantom{(\delta\mathbf{Y}^{N,(*)}_{T}=0)}}{=}\|(\delta Y^{i,N,(*)},\delta Z^{i,N,(*)},\delta U^{i,N,(*)},\delta M^{i,N,(*)})\|_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}}

\displaystyle\overset{(\delta\mathbf{Y}^{N,(*)}_{T}=0)}{\leq}\widehat{M}^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}\delta f^{i,N,(*)}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,i}_{\hat{\beta}}}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ineq:mf_sum_norm_delta_f_initial_path}}{\underset{\phantom{(\delta\mathbf{Y}^{N,(*)}_{T}=0}}{\leq}}\max\Big\{4,\frac{2}{\hat{\beta}}\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}\Big\}\widehat{M}^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\big\|\big(\delta\mathbf{y}^{N,(*)},\delta\mathbf{z}^{N,(*)},\delta\mathbf{u}^{N,(*)},\delta\mathbf{m}^{N,(*)}\big)\big\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}\times_{\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}},N}},

which provides the desired contraction.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

Let us provide at this point some comments related to the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

(i)

In order to control the Wasserstein distance between empirical measures, we derived

\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}^{2}\big(L^{N}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},1,N,(2)}_{s},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},N,N,(2)}_{s}),L^{N}(\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},1,N,(1)}_{s},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod},N,N,(1)}_{s})\big)\\
\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\Big[\sup_{r\in[0,s]}|\delta y^{m,N,(*)}_{r}|^{2}+|(\delta z_{s}^{m,N,(*)})c^{m}_{t}|^{2}+2\Big(\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\delta u_{s}^{m,N,(*)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|^{m}_{s}\Big)^{2}\Big].

The reader may observe that no expectation appears on the right-hand side of the inequality, which is something to be expected because of the nature of the left-hand side.
Indeed, there we have a Wasserstein distance of empirical measures, which depends on
\omega
.
Hence, in general, there cannot exist a deterministic upper bound for this random variable.

(ii)

It is easy to show that

L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}|_{[0,s]}^{\text{mod},N})
is a
\mathbb{G}-
optional process; see also the proof of
Lemma

A.9
for similar arguments.

(iii)

In the derivation of (
4.1.3
) (and of (
4.16
)) one faces the problem of multiplying the running maximum of processes lying within
\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i},f^{N})};\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the stochastic exponential associated to
A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{m},f^{N})}
, for
m\neq i
.
In general, one cannot derive such estimates, except for special cases; this is the purpose of introducing Condition
(MF4)
.
(MF4)(i)
-
(MF4)(ii)
.

(iv)

In view of
Remark

4.17
.
(iv)
, we may also consider to prove the existence and uniqueness of the solution of (
1
) and (

MF
Inst

¯
Y

) under a condition that involves the mean of the stochastic exponentials, however that is not possible.
This can be easily explained.
To do this, we must recall and combine two facts.
The first is the previous remark
(iii)
.
The second one is that due to (
4.27
) the Wasserstein distance provides an expectation, which allows us to integrate it with respect to a stochastic exponential, thus factorizing the respective mean values.
However, this is not the case for the inequality before (
4.1.3
) (and of (
4.16
)).

Proof of
Theorem

4.3
.

Essentially, the Proof of
Theorem

4.2
can be followed verbatim with the following change:
In the derivation of (
4.1.3
), (
4.2
) is replaced by (
4.3
) in view of the choice
x\overset{h}{\mapsto}\sqrt{x}
; recall
Remark

4.1
.
(i)
.
Hence, the assumption
\alpha\geq 1
imposed in
Theorem

4.2
becomes redundant.
Moreover, the presence in the empirical measure solely of the
\mathbb{Y}-
part yields
(MF4)
.
(MF4)(ii)
also redundant.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.4
.

Again, the Proof of
Theorem

4.2
can be followed verbatim with the following changes:

Obviously the initial, up to time
\cdot
segments of the paths of the
Y-
parts in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
should be substituted by the instantaneous values at
\cdot
,
e.g.
,
y^{i,N}|_{[0,\cdot]}
should be substituted by
y^{i,N}_{\cdot}
,
etc.

Under the assumption that
y^{N},y^{N,(1)},y^{N,(2)}\in\mathscr{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta},N}
, the essential change, now, is that in the derivation of (
4.1.3
) Assumption
(MF4)
.
(MF4)(i)
is not used, and we, therefore, need to deal with the
\mathbb{H}^{2}-
norm of the
\mathbb{Y}-
parts of the solution.
Given the fact that this norm cannot, in general, uniquely identify the paths of the integrand process, we choose to extend the
\mathbb{H}^{2}-
norms, which leads to the use of the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{S}^{2}}-
norms and consequently of the
\|\cdot\|_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}-
norms.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.5
.

The Proof of
Theorem

4.3
can be followed verbatim with the following obvious change: the initial, up to time
\cdot
segments of the paths of the
Y-
parts in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
should be substituted by the instantaneous values at
\cdot
,
e.g.
,
y^{i,N}|_{[0,\cdot]}
should be substituted by
y^{i,N}_{\cdot}
,
etc.

4.2.
McKean–Vlasov BSDEs

4.2.1.
Framework and structure of McKean–Vlasov BSDEs

Let us now introduce the setting for the existence and uniqueness theorems related to McKean–Vlasov BSDEs,
i.e.
BSDEs where the law of (part of the) solution affects the generator of the equation.
Let us fix a stochastic basis
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P})
, a
\mathbb{G}-
stopping time
T
and
\hat{\beta}>0
.
The stochastic basis supports the following:

(MV1)

The pair of martingales
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}[\Delta X^{\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}]=0
.
16
16

16

Since the filtration
\mathbb{G}
is given as well as the pair
\mathbf{X}
, we will make use of
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
, resp.

c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
, as defined in (
2.9
), resp. (
2.10
).
Moreover, we will use the kernels
K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
as determined by (
2.11
).

(MV2)

The generator
f:\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{X})\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}

where
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})
and
(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})
are metric spaces.
17
17

17

As in
subsection

4.1
,
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})
will be either
(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
or
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\rho_{|\cdot|})
, while
\mathscr{X}
will be either
\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
\mathbb{X}
.

The generator is, additionally, such that for any
(y,z,u,\mu)\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{X})
the map

\displaystyle(\omega,t)\longmapsto f(\omega,t,y,z,u,\mu)\text{ is }\mathbb{G}-\text{progressively measurable}

and satisfies the following (stochastic) Lipschitz condition: for any
(y,z,u,\mu)
,
(y^{\prime},z^{\prime},u^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\in\mathbb{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{X})
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}|f(\omega,t,y,z,u,\mu)-f(\omega,t,y^{\prime},z^{\prime},u^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})|^{2}\\
\leq r(\omega,t)\rho_{\mathbb{X}}(y,y^{\prime})^{2}+\vartheta^{\circ}(\omega,t)|z-z^{\prime}|^{2}\\
+\vartheta^{\natural}(\omega,t)|u-u^{\prime}|^{2}+\vartheta^{*}(\omega,t)\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\left(\mu,\mu^{\prime}\right),\end{multlined}

where
(r,\vartheta^{\circ},\vartheta^{\natural},\vartheta^{*}):\left(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathcal{P}^{\mathbb{G}}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{+},\mathcal{B}\left(\mathbb{R}^{4}_{+}\right)\right)
.

(MV3)

The
\mathbb{G}
–predictable and càdlàg process

\displaystyle A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{\cdot}:=\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha^{2}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s},

is such that there exists
\Phi>0
with

\displaystyle\Delta A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{t}(\omega)\leq\Phi,\ \mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}-\text{a.e.},

where
\alpha^{2}_{\cdot}:=\max\{\sqrt{r_{\cdot}},\vartheta^{\circ}_{\cdot},\vartheta^{\natural}_{\cdot},h(\vartheta^{*}_{\cdot})\}
, with
\alpha^{2}>0
, for a function
h
to be specified.
18
18

18

For notational simplicity we have suppressed the dependence on
(\mathbb{G},f)
in the notation of
\alpha
, but we have carried it in the notation of
A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}
. Moreover, we suppress the dependence on the choice of the function
h
as in the statement of each theorem this function will be fixed.

(MV4)

(i)

There exists
\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}>0
such that
\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}\big)_{T}\leq\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}

\mathbb{P}-
a.s.

(ii)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}\big)_{T}\Big]<\infty
.

(iii)

The processes
\alpha
and
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
are deterministic.
19
19

19

This immediately give us that the process
A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}
is also deterministic.

(MV5)

There exist functions
\Theta
and
\Gamma
as in
Definition

2.7
, where the data for the definition are the pair
(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})
, the process
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
and the kernels
K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
.

(MV6)

The terminal condition satisfies
\xi\in\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathcal{G}_{T},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(MV7)

\big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f(\cdot,0,0,0,\delta_{0})\big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{G},A^{\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)},C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})};\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty
, where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac measure concentrated at
0
, the neutral element of the addition for the space
\mathscr{X}.

Let us now present the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs we will consider; the cases for
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})
and
(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})
will be the same as in
subsection

4.1
, and we will follow analogous rationale in tagging the equations.
This generality allows us to describe the problem of solving a McKean–Vlasov BSDE as seeking a process
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}:=(Y,Z,U,M)
with

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}\in\mathcal{S}^{2}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)},X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})\\
\times\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)},\mu^{X^{\natural}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)},\mathbf{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{G}}};\mathbb{R}^{d}),\end{multlined}

and such that

(MV)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y_{t}=\xi+\int^{T}_{t}f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}})\big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s},\end{multlined}

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}
is a suitable “modification” (not to be understood in the probabilistic sense) of
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}
.
20
20

20

The modification is not needed when
\mathscr{X}\equiv\mathbb{X}
; see
Theorem

4.11

Let us return to the two main cases, depending on the choice of the metric space
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})
, that we will consider in this work and provide the remaining details.

On the one hand, when
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
, we consider two subcases:
(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
and
(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p},\rho_{\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}})
.
In the latter case, the McKean–Vlasov BSDE (
MV
) takes the form

(
\textrm{MV}_{\textrm{Seg}}^{\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}}
)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y_{t}=\xi+\int^{T}_{t}f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}_{s})\big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X}^{i})}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\end{multlined}

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y|_{[0,\cdot]},Z_{\cdot}c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{\cdot},\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{\cdot}\big).

In the former case, the McKean–Vlasov BSDE (
MV
) takes the form, for
i\in\mathscr{N}
,

(
\textrm{MV}_{\textrm{Seg}}^{Y}
)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y_{t}=\xi+\int^{T}_{t}f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}},\mathcal{L}(Y|_{[0,s]})\big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\end{multlined}

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y|_{[0,\cdot]},Z_{\cdot}c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{\cdot},\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{\cdot}\big).

In other words, in the first case, the BSDE depends on the law of all the components of the solution process, while in the second one only on the law of
Y
; in both cases, it also depends on the whole segment
[0,\cdot]
of the processes.

On the other hand, when
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})=(\mathbb{R}^{d},\rho_{|\cdot|})
, we consider the two subcases:

(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p},\rho_{\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}})

and

(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
.
In the former case, the McKean–Vlasov BSDE (
MV
) takes the form

(
1
)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y_{t}=\xi+\int^{T}_{t}f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}_{s})\big)\Big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\end{multlined}

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y_{\cdot},Z_{\cdot}c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{\cdot},\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{\cdot}\big).

In the latter case, the McKean–Vlasov BSDE (
MV
) takes the form, for
i\in\mathscr{N}
,

(
\textrm{MV}_{\textrm{Inst}}^{Y}
)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}Y_{t}=\xi+\int^{T}_{t}f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}},\mathcal{L}(Y_{s})\big)\,\mathrm{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\\
-\int^{T}_{t}Z_{s}\,\mathrm{d}X^{\circ}_{s}-\int^{T}_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{s}(x)\,\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}x)-\int^{T}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}M_{s}\end{multlined}

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod}}_{\cdot}:=\big(Y_{\cdot},Z_{\cdot}c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{\cdot},\Gamma^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},\Theta)}(U)_{\cdot}\big).

Now, in the first case, the BSDE depends on the law of all the components of the solution process, while in the second one only on the law of
Y
; in both cases, it also depends on the instantaneous value of the processes.

4.2.3.
Proofs of the McKean–Vlasov BSDE theorems

In order to proceed with the proofs of the stated theorems, we will initially use the proof of
Theorem

4.2
as a reference in order to minimize replication of the required arguments.
To this end, the reader should consider the following “simplifications” as a starting point:
i=N=1
, and it is the law of the respective part of the solution in place of the empirical measure.
We underline that even in this simple case where
i=N=1
, the empirical measure is a random measure, while the law that appears in the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs generator is deterministic.
Thus, the following change applies to all of the following cases: in the derivation of (
4.1.3
), and consequently of (
4.16
) as well, the estimation (
2.31
) should be replaced by the following,

(4.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}^{2}\big(\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{1}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{2})\big)\leq\mathbb{E}\big[\rho_{\mathscr{X}}^{2}(\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{2})\big],

for
\mathscr{X}-
valued random variables
\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{1},\widetilde{\mathbf{T}}^{2}
.
Notationally, we drop all indices
i
and
N
and replace
\overline{\mathbf{s}}
,
\overline{\mathbf{S}}
,
etc.
, by
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}
, and so forth.
Additionally, for notational simplicity, we also write
A
instead of
A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}
,
etc
.

Proof of Theorem
4.10
.

In view of the previous remarks, on the one hand, (
4.1.3
) now reads

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f\left(\cdot,\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod}}_{\cdot},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}^{\text{mod}}_{\cdot})\right)\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\underset{\phantom{\ref{MV_A_cases}.\ref{MV_StochExp_Bound}}}{\overset{\eqref{properties_stoch_bounds}}{\underset{\eqref{properties_stoch_bounds_onlyWasserstein_identity}}{\leq}}}2\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f\big(\cdot,0,0,0,\delta_{0}\big)\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{s-})\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|y_{u}|^{2}\textup{d}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{s}\Big]\\
+2\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{s-})\mathbb{E}[\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|y_{u}|^{2}]\textup{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\Big]+4\|z\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+8\|u\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\end{multlined}

(4.28)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\overset{\ref{MV_A_cases}.\ref{MV_StochExp_Bound}}{\leq}2\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f\big(\cdot,0,0,0,\delta_{0}\big)\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}}+\frac{4\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\|y\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}}+4\|z\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+8\|u\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\overset{\ref{MV_Integrability_Generator}}{\underset{\phantom{\ref{MV_A_cases}.\ref{MV_StochExp_Bound}}}{<}}\infty,

where in the second inequality we used the fact that
\alpha^{2}\geq 1
.
On the other hand, (
4.16
) now reads

\displaystyle\hskip-10.00002pt\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}\delta f^{(*)}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{s-})\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|\delta y^{(*)}_{u}\big|^{2}\textup{d}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{s}\Big]+2\|\delta z^{(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+4\|\delta u^{(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\\
+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{s-})\mathbb{E}[\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|\delta y^{(*)}_{u}|^{2}]\textup{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\Big]\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq\frac{2\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\|\delta y^{(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\|\delta z^{(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\circ}_{\hat{\beta}}}+4\|\delta u^{(*)}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2,\natural}_{\hat{\beta}}}\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq\max\Big\{4,\frac{2}{\hat{\beta}}\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}\Big\}\big\|\big(\delta\mathbf{y}^{(*)},\delta\mathbf{z}^{(*)},\delta\mathbf{u}^{(*)},\delta\mathbf{m}^{(*)}\big)\big\|^{2}_{\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}\times\mathscr{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}},\end{multlined}

where in the second inequality we used the fact that
\alpha^{2}\geq 1
.
Thus, the contraction constant derived from above is

\max\big\{4,\frac{2}{\hat{\beta}}\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}\big\}\widehat{M}^{\Phi}_{\star}
which is assumed to be smaller than 1.
∎

Proof of Theorem

.

used in the previous theorem.
Then, (

) improve their coefficients.
As a result, the contraction constant is
\max\big\{2,\frac{2}{\hat{\beta}}\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}\big\}\widehat{M}^{\Phi}_{\star}
which is assumed to be smaller than 1.
∎

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\underset{\phantom{\ref{MV_A_cases}.\ref{MV_StochExp_Integrable}}}{\overset{\eqref{properties_stoch_bounds}}{\underset{\eqref{properties_stoch_bounds_onlyWasserstein_identity}}{\leq}}}2\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}f\big(\cdot,0,0,0,\delta_{0}\big)\Big\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}}+2\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)}_{s-})\alpha_{s}^{2}|y_{s}|^{2}\textup{d}C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}_{s}\Big]\\
