Remark 4.17
.

Let us now provide some remarks related to specific points of the previous proofs:

(i)

If
T
is deterministic and finite, then Conditions
(MV4)
.
(MV4)(i)
,
(MV4)(ii)
are automatically satisfied. However, when
T=\infty
these does not necessarily hold in general.

(ii)

Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}^{2}\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathscr{H}_{\hat{\beta}}^{2}
, then from (
2.27
) we have that
\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{s}}|_{[0,\cdot]}^{\text{mod}})
is a (deterministic) measurable process, indeed this holds because (
\mathbb{X}:=\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
) for every continuous and bounded function
h:\mathbb{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, the deterministic process
\int_{\mathbb{X}}h(x)\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{s}}|_{[0,t]}^{\text{mod}})(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\Omega}h({\mathbf{s}}|_{[0,t]}^{\text{mod}})\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{d}\omega)
is measurable by Fubini’s theorem.

(iii)

The imposition of the bound
\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}
seems inevitable if we want to consider path-dependent BSDEs, i.e. those whose generator
f
depends on the initial segment of the paths of the solution
Y
. Indeed, in the proofs of
Theorems

4.10
and
4.11
we need to multiply the stochastic exponential with the (square of the) running maximum of
y^{2}-y^{1}
.
One could possibl, be able to proceed without an assumption on boundedness of the stochastic exponential, if it were possible to extract
a priori
estimates for the running maximum analogous to
Lemma

3.1
.
In this case, one expects in
Lemma

3.1
an integrability condition of the form

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)})_{T-}\sup_{s\in[0,T]}|y_{s}|^{2}]<\infty
to appear, which is clearly stronger than the
\mathcal{S}^{2}-
norm we are using.
However, in this work, we have not pursued such
a priori
estimates.

(iv)

For the BSDEs (
4.2.1
) and (

MV
Inst

Y

) whose generator

depends at time

on the instantaneous value of the solution
Y
,
e.g.

or

, and not on the initial - up to time
s
- segment of its paths,
i.e.
, on

or

, we could proceed under the assumption that
\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)})_{T}
is integrable, for the
\hat{\beta}
determining standard data.
However, in this case we needed to seek a solution related to the
\|\cdot\|_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}
-norm instead of the

-norm
such that

\displaystyle Y\in\mathcal{S}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)};\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{ and }\quad\alpha Y\in\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{G},A^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X},f)},C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})};\mathbb{R}^{d}),

instead of simply

.

5.
Backward propagation of chaos in a general setting: the stochastic Lipschitz case

In this section, we will present general results on the propagation of chaos for particles that satisfy a backward SDE, as announced in the introduction of this work.
The technique for our proof is novel and differs from the classical results in the literature, in that we make repeated use of the
a priori
estimates and the Strong Law of Large Numbers, instead of using a delicate change of measure and an application of Girsanov’s theorem or some form of Gronwall’s inequality or Itō’s lemma, as in
Laurière and Tangpi [45]
and the literature on the propagation of chaos for forward SDEs.
This method allows us to consider a more general setting, and we work with asymmetric mean-field systems and general square integrable drivers with jumps.

Despite the fact that we have already provided existence and uniqueness results for the respective mean-field BSDE systems and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs, there are still quite a few preparatory and auxiliary results that will be required before we present the proof of the respective propagation of chaos statements.
These auxiliary results are presented here, while their proofs are deferred to
subsections

A.2
and
A.3
.

Let us recall that stochastic processes are typically associated with a reference filtration.
More specifically, ‘compensator-type’ processes are defined with respect to a predictable
\sigma-
algebra, which depends on the reference filtration.
In the propagation of chaos statements, on the one hand we want the solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs to be independent; hence, we solve each one in its own filtration.
On the other hand, when we solve the mean-field systems of BSDEs we work in filtrations larger than those of the corresponding McKean–Vlasov BSDEs.
Therefore, the question naturally arises whether the solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs with respect to the smaller filtrations remain solutions when we work in the larger filtrations.
The appendices
A.2
and
A.3
provide the tools necessary to prove such ‘conservation of solutions’ type results under the condition of immersion of filtrations.

Moreover, as expected, the framework we are going to use for the propagation of chaos will be based on the common ground of the frameworks we used in the previous sections, suitably enriched and reinforced wherever required.

5.1.
Setting

Let
T\in(0,\infty]
be a deterministic time,
\hat{\beta}>0
and
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space which supports the following:

Remark 5.1
.

(i)

By construction,
\{\mathbb{F}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of independent filtrations on
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{P})
.
Moreover, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}:=\bigvee_{m=1}^{N}\mathbb{F}^{m}
.
Using
He and Wang [33, Theorem 1]
we have that
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
satisfies the usual conditions.
Let
i\in\mathscr{N}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
then,
a direct consequence of the independence of filtrations is that every
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
martingale, remains martingale under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
,
i.e.
, the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{i}
is immersed in the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
.
In particular,
X^{i,\natural}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}};\mathbb{R}^{n})
; see
Corollary

A.6
.
Additionally, from the assumption
M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}[\Delta X^{i,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{i}}]=0
one can deduce that
M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}[\Delta X^{i,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}}]=0
also holds; one can follow the exact same arguments as in
Lemma

A.8
.

(ii)

Let
i\in\mathscr{N}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Then due to the independence of
\{\mathbb{F}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have that
\mathbb{F}^{i}
is immersed in
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
, and
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
is immersed in
\bigvee_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{F}^{m}
.

(iii)

The sequence of driving martingales associated to the McKean–Vlasov equations are copies of one another (I.I.D.), and the same holds for the terminal random variables.
Hence, from
Lemma

A.8
(see also
subsection

A.1
), we can view the solutions also as copies of one another, in other words, as strong solutions under the larger filtration and conclude their uniqueness in law.
In other words, the solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs are identically distributed.
We note that the same holds true for the processes
\{\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{i},\mathbf{X}^{i},\Theta)}(U^{i})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
; recall the representation of
\Gamma
provided in
Remark

2.8

(iii)
.

(iv)

Combining
(PC2)
and
(PC7)
we get

\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s-}\frac{1}{\alpha_{s}^{2}}|f(s,0,0,0,\delta_{0})|^{2}\textup{d}C_{s}<\infty.

Proposition 5.2
.

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a filtration on
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{P})
that satisfies the usual conditions.
Consider a pair
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with independent increments.
Then, the processes
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
and
c^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
, as defined via (
2.9
) and (
2.10
), are deterministic.

Proof.

Let
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, then
X^{\circ,j}
denotes the
j-
element of the
p-
dimensional process
X^{\circ}
.
Analogously,
x^{j}
denotes the
j-
element of the
p-
dimensional vector
x
.
Using
He et al. [32, Definition 6.27]
, we have that the dual predictable projection of the process
\sum_{s\leq t}|\Delta X^{\circ,j}_{s}|^{2}
is equal to
(x^{j})^{2}*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\circ})}
, for every
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
.
Then, using
Medvegyev [51, Corollaries 7.87, 7.88]
, we get that
\langle X^{\circ}\rangle^{\mathbb{G}}
and
\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}
are deterministic.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The independence of the increments of the martingale
\mathbf{X}
is equivalent to its associated triplet being deterministic; see
Medvegyev [51, Corollary 7.87]
or
Jacod and Shiryaev [39, Theorem II.4.15]
.

5.2.
Statements of the backward propagation of chaos theorems

In this subsection, we will present propagation of chaos theorems between systems of mean-field BSDEs (
MF
) and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs (
MV
), for different cases, depending on whether the driver of the BSDE depends on all the components of the BSDE solution or just
Y
, and whether this dependence includes only the instantaneous value of the processes or the whole initial segment
[0,\cdot]
.
Let us fix
\hat{k}\in\mathbb{N}
, then we will focus on the first
\hat{k}
players of the mean-field system and prove that their asymptotic behavior is the one dictated by the respective McKean–Vlasov dynamics.

Theorem 5.4

(Propagation of chaos)
.

Let
\hat{k}\in\mathbb{N}
. Under
(PC1)
,
(PC2)
for
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
and
(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p},\rho_{\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}})
,
(PC3)
for
[0,\infty)\ni x\overset{h}{\mapsto}x
and
\alpha^{2}_{\cdot}\geq 1
,
(PC4)
,
(PC5)
,
(PC6)
and
(PC7)
, if

5.3.
Proofs of the backward propagation of chaos theorems

Our approach is based on the strong comparison,
i.e.
, under the norm, of the solutions of mean-field systems of BSDEs and McKean–Vlasov BSDEs.
This requires to embed these BSDEs under a common, large enough, filtration.
Consequently, when we refer to a specific BSDE, we often need to distinguish under which filtration we consider this.
Of course, a crucial question is whether the actual solution remains the same under a change of filtration or not.
This has an affirmative answer under specific circumstances; see
subsection

A.3
.
We have already introduced the notation
\mathbb{F}^{i}
, for
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
, for
N\in\mathbb{N}
where
\mathscr{N}:=\{1,\dots,n\}
; see
(PC1)
and
(PC7)
.
Let us also introduce the notation
\mathbb{F}^{\mathbb{N}}:=\bigvee_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{F}^{i}
.
Thus, under this framework, the solution of a mean-field BSDE system remains the same under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
and under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathbb{N}}
, while the solution of the McKean–Vlasov BSDE for the
i-
th player remains the same under
\mathbb{F}^{i}
, under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
, and under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Moreover, for any object
Q
indexed in both
\{i,N\}
and
N
we introduce the following notation

(5.1)

\displaystyle\delta^{N}Q^{i}:=Q^{i,N}-Q^{i}.

Proof of
Theorem

5.4
.

The proof will be divided into multiple steps, so let us briefly describe them.
Initially, we will provide an estimate for the normed difference between the solutions of the mean-field and the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs; this estimate utilizes
Lemma

3.1
.
Then, at each subsequent step, we will prove the convergence of each term appearing on the upper bound of this estimate.
Let us point out that the order of the subsequent steps should be chosen wisely, because the computations in each one depend on preceding results.
This way, we avoid the repetition of certain computations.

Let
N\geq\hat{k}
.
Let
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}=(\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N})_{i\in\mathscr{N}}
be the solution of the mean-field BSDE under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
and

\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}
be the solution of McKean–Vlasov BSDE under
\mathbb{F}^{i}
.
We underline that, in view of
Lemma

A.8
, the aforementioned solutions remain solutions of the respective BSDEs under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
or even under
\mathbb{F}^{\mathbb{N}}
.
This property will be used to embed the processes into a common filtered space, enabling an immediate and strong comparison.

Using the notation introduced above, let us consider the difference between the mean-field and the McKean–Vlasov BSDE

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\delta^{N}Y^{i}_{t}=Y^{i,N}_{t}-Y^{i}_{t}=\delta^{N}\xi^{i}\\
+\int^{T}_{t}\Big\{f^{i,N}\big(s,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N}_{s},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s})\big)-f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})\big)\Big\}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}\\
-\int_{t}^{T}\,\mathrm{d}\Big(\left(\delta^{N}Z^{i}\right)\cdot X^{i,\circ}+\left(\delta^{N}U^{i}\right)\star{\widetilde{\mu}}^{(\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},X^{i,\natural})}+\delta^{N}M^{i}\Big)_{s},\end{multlined}

which crucially yields another system of BSDEs; later we will apply the a priori estimates to this system as well.
Let us introduce the following auxiliary notation:

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\psi^{i,N}

\displaystyle:=f^{i,N}\big(s,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N})\big)-f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i})\big),

\displaystyle\psi^{i,N,[1]}

\displaystyle:=f^{i,N}\big(s,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N})\big)-f\big(s,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N})\big),

\displaystyle\psi^{i,N,[2]}

\displaystyle:=f\big(s,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i,N},L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N})\big)-f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i},L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N})\big)

and

\displaystyle\psi^{i,N,[3]}

\displaystyle:=f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i},L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N})\big)-f\big(s,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i})\big),

that will be utilized in the proof later.
Applying
Lemma

3.1
to (
5.3
), we obtain, for every
i\in\mathscr{N}\equiv\{1,\dots,N\}
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
, an estimate of the form
30
30

30

For ease of notation we avoid the detailed description of the norms as it is clear from the context which norm is used.

\displaystyle\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\xi}\|\delta^{N}\xi^{i}\|^{2}+M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\Big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}\psi^{i,N}\Big\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\xi}\|\delta^{N}\xi^{i}\|^{2}+3M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\Big(\big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}\psi^{i,N,[1]}\big\|^{2}+\big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}\psi^{i,N,[2]}\big\|^{2}+\big\|\frac{1}{\alpha}\psi^{i,N,[3]}\big\|^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\leq C_{\xi}\|\delta^{N}\xi^{i}\|^{2}+\frac{3}{\hat{\beta}}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}g^{i,N}_{s}\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)_{s}\Big]\\
+\max\left\{6,\frac{3\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\Big\{\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{m,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{m}\|^{2}\Big\}\\
+\frac{6}{\hat{\beta}}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})\big)\Big]\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)_{s},\end{multlined}

Let us point out that the right-hand side of (
5.3
) also depends on
\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}
.
Therefore, we will need to obtain some auxiliary results for the desired conclusion.
These will be presented as intermediate steps:

Step 1
:

We will first show that, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

(5.4)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})\big)\Big]\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)_{s}\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0,

which settles the convergence of the last term on the right-hand-side of (
5.3
).
This proof will be decomposed into three subtasks.
In the first one, using the law of large numbers we will prove, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, that

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})\big)\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0\quad\mathbb{P}-a.s.\text{ for every }s\in[0,T].

In the second one, we will show, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in[0,T]
, that

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})\big)\Big]\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0.

In the third one, we will deduce the existence of a measurable and integrable function such that for every
i,N\in\mathbb{N}
it dominates the measurable function

\displaystyle[0,T]\ni s\mapsto\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})\big)\Big].

Thus, by means of the dominated convergence theorem we will conclude the convergence (
5.4
).

Let us recall a simple, yet crucial remark:
the solution of the McKean–Vlasov
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
BSDE (

MV
Seg

~
S

mod

) is an independent copy of the McKean–Vlasov
\mathbb{F}^{j}-
BSDE (

MV
Seg

~
S

mod

), for every
i\neq j
with
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
.
This is the outcome of the independence of the filtrations
\mathbb{F}^{i}
and
\mathbb{F}^{j}
generated respectively by the (independent) integrators
\mathbf{X}^{i}
and
\mathbf{X}^{j}
, for every
i\neq j
with
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
, in conjunction with
Lemma

A.8
.
The latter allows to recognize the aforementioned solutions as strong solutions under the larger filtration
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
, or even
\mathbb{F}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and conclude that their laws are identical.
Thus, in particular, we have for every
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
and
s\in[0,T]
that
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})=\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},j}_{s})
31
31

31

Being more precise, we remind the reader that the objects
c,C
and
K
are deterministic; see
Footnote

23
.
.
Therefore, it is sufficient to prove all the above for
i=1
.

Let us present the arguments for the validity of the first task described above,
i.e.
, the validity of (
5.5
).
Essentially, we apply
Villani [60, Definition 6.8 (iv) and Theorem 6.9]
, with
x_{0}=0
,
Lemma

2.12
and the aforementioned remark to get from the Strong Law of Large Numbers the desired result.
More precisely, we might recall the comments provided in
subsection

2.9
, and, in particular, the fact that the Wasserstein distance metrizes the weak convergence of measures in
\mathscr{P}_{r}(\mathscr{X})
, which allows to translate it to the more standard convergence of integrals, known from the usual weak convergence of measures in
\mathscr{P}(\mathscr{X})
. Fix
s\in[0,T]
, from
Lemma

2.12
we only need to show - we follow the notation of
Lemma

2.12
- that for a suitable sequence
\big\{f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})}_{k}\big\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq C(\mathscr{X})
such that

\displaystyle|f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})}_{k}(x)|\leq C(1+\rho_{\mathscr{X}}(0,x)^{2}),

for a constant
C>0
which depends on
f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})}_{k}
, it holds

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{\mathscr{X}}f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})}_{k}\,\textup{d}L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s})=\int_{\mathscr{X}}f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})}_{k}\,\textup{d}\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})\,\mathbb{P}-a.s.,

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the above equality is equivalently written as

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},m}_{s})=\mathbb{E}\left[f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})\right]\mathbb{P}-a.s.,

which, in fact, is the Strong Law of Large Numbers for the independent and identically distributed random variables

\Big(f^{\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1})}_{k}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},m})\Big)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
; see
Varadarajan [59, Theorem 3]
.

Next, we proceed with the verification of (
5.6
). To this end, let us initially provide some preparatory estimates.
Namely, by applying the triangle inequality for the Wasserstein distance as well as (
2.31
) we get that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}^{2}\Big(L^{N}\big(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}\big),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq 2\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}^{2}\left(L^{N}\big(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}\big),\delta_{0}\right)+2\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}^{2}\big(\delta_{0},\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})\big)

(5.7)

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\begin{multlined}\leq 2\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\bigg[\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|Y^{m}_{u}|^{2}+\|Z^{m}_{s}c_{s}\|^{2}+|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{m},\mathbf{X}^{m},\Theta)}(U^{m})_{s}|^{2}\bigg]\\
\hskip 14.22636pt+2\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|Y^{1}_{u}|^{2}+\|Z^{1}_{s}c_{s}\|^{2}+|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{s}|^{2}\right].\end{multlined}

For the validity of the desired claim, it is sufficient to prove that the right-hand side of (
5.7
) is uniformly integrable.
This would imply the uniform integrability of the left-hand side, as dominated by a uniformly integrable one.
Using that
\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{\cdot}\big)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is identically distributed, then
a fortiori
the sequences
(\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|Y^{m}_{u}|^{2})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(\|Z^{m}_{s}c_{s}\|^{2})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{m},\mathbf{X}^{m},\Theta)}(U^{m})_{s}|^{2})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
are identically distributed.
Additionally, by the finiteness of the norms, the aforementioned sequences are integrable, for every
s\in[0,T]
.
Combining the above it is, in particular, implied the uniform integrability of the respective sequences.
We now conclude the desired uniform integrability of the right-hand side of (
5.7
), using the fact that sums of uniformly integrable families remain uniformly integrable, together with the fact that the convex hull of a uniformly integrable family is also uniformly integrable.

Finally, we need to verify that the dominated convergence theorem can be indeed applied.
Using the information presented in the above lines for every
s\in[0,T]
and by applying the expectation on (
5.7
) we have

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},i}_{s})\big)\Big]\\
\leq 4\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|Y^{1}_{u}|^{2}+\|Z^{1}_{s}c_{s}\|^{2}+|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{s}|^{2}\Big].\end{multlined}

Furthermore, we have for the right-hand side of the estimate above

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|Y^{1}_{u}|^{2}+\|Z^{1}_{s}c_{s}\|^{2}+|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{s}|^{2}\Big]\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}\\
\leq\hat{\beta}\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}\|\left(Y^{1},Z^{1},U^{1},M^{1}\right)\|^{2}_{\star,\hat{\beta},\mathbb{F}^{1},\alpha,C,\mathbf{X}^{1}}.\end{multlined}

Hence, the Borel–measurable function

[0,T]\ni s\longmapsto 4\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{u\in[0,s]}|Y^{1}_{u}|^{2}+\|Z^{1}_{s}c_{s}\|^{2}+|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{s}|^{2}\right]

is
\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)-
integrable and dominates the sequence

\displaystyle\Big([0,T]\ni s\mapsto\mathbb{E}\Big[W_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}^{2}\big(L^{N}\big(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}\big),\mathcal{L}\big(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s}\big)\big)\Big]\Big)_{N\in\mathbb{N}}.

Step 2
:

Under the condition
\max\left\{12,\frac{6\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})<1
, we will prove that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{m,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{m}\|\xrightarrow[N\to\infty]{}0.

Indeed, summing (
5.3
) over
i\in\mathscr{N}
and dividing by
N
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\|

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\leq\frac{C_{\xi}}{1-\max\left\{12,\frac{6\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|\delta^{N}\xi^{i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\frac{3M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}\Big(1-\max\left\{12,\frac{6\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\Big)}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}g^{i,N}_{s}\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)_{s}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt+\frac{6M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}\Big(1-\max\left\{12,\frac{6\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\Big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})\big)\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)_{s}\Big],

which provides the desired convergence in view of
(PC6)
,
(PC2)
.
(PC2)(iii)
and
Step 1
.

Step 3
:

Returning to (
5.3
) and summing over
\{1,\dots,\hat{k}\}
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\hat{k}}\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\xi}}{1-\max\left\{6,\frac{3\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})}\sum_{i=1}^{\hat{k}}\|\delta^{N}\xi^{i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{3M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}\Big(1-\max\left\{6,\frac{3\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\Big)}\sum_{i=1}^{\hat{k}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}g^{i,N}_{s}\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)_{s}\Big]

\displaystyle+\frac{\max\left\{6,\frac{3\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\hat{k}}{1-\max\left\{6,\frac{3\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{m,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{m}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{6M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\hat{k}}{\hat{\beta}\Big(1-\max\left\{6,\frac{3\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}M^{\Phi}_{\star}(\hat{\beta})\Big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X}}}\big(L^{N}(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s})\big)\textup{d}\mathcal{E}(\hat{\beta}A)_{s}\Big],

which provides the desired convergence in view of
(PC6)
,
(PC2)
.
(PC2)(iii)
,
Step 2
and
Step 1
.

∎

5.4.
Immediate corollaries

In this subsection, we will present some immediate corollaries of the backward propagation of chaos theorems.
In order to ease the presentation, we will introduce an auxiliary notation.
Namely, for
\hat{k}\in\mathbb{N}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
N\geq\hat{k}
we will denote by
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}_{\cdot}|_{\hat{k}}
the part of the solution
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}_{\cdot}
that is associated with the first
\hat{k}
players of the
N-
player mean field BSDE system,
i.e.
,
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}_{\cdot}|_{\hat{k}}:=(\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{1,N}_{\cdot},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\hat{k},N}_{\cdot})
.
Analogously,

\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{\cdot}|_{\hat{k}}:=(\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1,N}_{\cdot},\dots,\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},\hat{k},N}_{\cdot})
.
Let us recall that for the McKean–Vlasov solutions associated to the first
\hat{k}
players, we have

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\hat{k}}_{\cdot}:=(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{1}_{\cdot},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\hat{k}}_{\cdot})
and

\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},\hat{k}}_{\cdot}:=(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{\cdot},\dots,\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},\hat{k}}_{\cdot})
.

The following consist of the analogons of the usual convergence in law statements for the solutions of the mean-field systems to the solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs.

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
\hat{k}\leq N\in\mathbb{N}
.
The following are true for any set of assumptions of Theorems
5.4
-
5.7
:

(i)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\Big\|\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X},\hat{k}}}\Big(\mathcal{L}\big(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{\cdot}|_{\hat{k}}\big),\mathcal{L}\big(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},\hat{k}}_{\cdot}\big)\Big)\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},A,C;\mathbb{R})}=0.

(ii)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{W}^{2}_{2,\mathbb{X}}\big(\mathcal{L}(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}|_{\hat{k}}),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}^{\hat{k}}_{t})\big)=0
, where, in case
\mathbb{X}=\mathbb{D}
, for each
t\in[0,T]
the initial segments (up to time
t
) of the paths are considered.

Proof.

We will only prove the case where
(\mathbb{X},\rho_{\mathbb{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d},\rho_{J_{1}})
and
(\mathscr{X},\rho_{\mathscr{X}})=(\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p},\rho_{\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{p}})
; the others can be treated analogously.
Let
t\in[0,T]
.
Then, by definition of the Wasserstein distance of order two, we have
32
32

32

The reader might recall the notation introduced in (
5.1
).

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}W^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X},\hat{k}}}\left(\mathcal{L}\big(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{t}|_{\hat{k}}\big),\mathcal{L}\big(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},\hat{k}}_{t}\big)\right)\leq\int_{(\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2\hat{k}}}\rho_{\mathscr{X},\hat{k}}(x,z)^{2}\pi(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}z)\\
\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\hat{k}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\{|\delta^{i}Y^{N}_{s}|^{2}\}+\|(\delta^{i}Z^{N}_{t})c_{t}\|^{2}+|\delta^{i}\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{X}^{i},\Theta)}(U^{N})_{t}|^{2}\Big].\end{multlined}

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\Big\|W^{2}_{2,\rho_{\mathscr{X},\hat{k}}}\Big(\mathcal{L}\big(\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{\cdot}|_{\hat{k}}\big),\mathcal{L}\big(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},\hat{k}}_{\cdot}\big)\Big)\Big\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},A,C;\mathbb{R})}\leq\max\left\{2,\frac{\Lambda_{\hat{\beta}}}{\hat{\beta}}\right\}\sum_{i=1}^{\hat{k}}\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\|^{2}_{\star}.

Therefore, by
Theorem

5.4
the proof of the first statement is complete.
The proof of the second statement follows using similar arguments and is therefore omitted.
∎

The next result,
Corollary

5.9
, is the analogon of
Laurière and Tangpi [45, Lemma 3.3]
.
These results consist of preparatory steps in order to derive convergence rates for the backward propagation of chaos.
Apart from their own independent interest, we would like to draw the reader’s attention to their proofs.
Namely, in
Corollary

5.9
we will essentially repeat the series of simple, but efficient, arguments as presented in the proofs of Theorems
5.4
-
5.7
; let us point out that the dependence of the generator can go beyond the empirical law of the
\overline{\mathbb{Y}}-
part of the solution.
Thus, no symmetry is needed with respect to the players’ position in the
N-
player game, as well as no classical techniques, such as Grönwall’s lemma and Girsanov’s theorem.
This means that the subadditive relationship as described in the proof of
Corollary

5.9
can be directly derived.
On the other hand, in
Laurière and Tangpi [45, Lemma 3.3]
the analogous subadditive relationship is modified in a multiplicative one, as a result of the application of Grönwall’s inequality.
However, the application of Girsanov’s theorem was required before the application of Grönwall’s inequality.
This point is quite delicate and requires extra caution as – following the notation of the Proof of
[
45
, Lem. 3.3, p. 14]
– the measure
Q
used for Girsanov’s theorem is connected to the
i-
th player.
However, the measure
Q
is finally used for every player, as the
E_{Q}-
conditional expectation is calculated for every player.
This point can be, possibly, remedied by means of the symmetry assumed in the mean-field system of
[
45
]
. In total, the series of arguments we propose has the advantage of simplicity, which allows for their application in more general settings where Grönwall’s inequality and/or Girsanov’s theorem may not be applicable.

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
t\in[0,T]
and consider any set of assumptions of Theorems
5.4
-
5.7
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}({Y}^{1}_{t})\big)\Big]=0.

Proof.

Using the triangle inequality of the Wasserstein distance and (
2.31
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}({Y}^{1}_{t})\big)\\
\leq 2\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}),L^{N}({\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}}^{N}_{t})\big)+2\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}({\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}}^{N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}({Y}^{1}_{t})\big)\\
\leq 2\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}|Y^{m,N}_{t}-Y^{m}_{t}|^{2}+2\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}\big({\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}}^{N}_{t}\big),\mathcal{L}\big(Y^{1}_{t}\big)\big).\end{multlined}

We can then conclude, using
Step 2
, resp.
Step 1
, of the proofs of Theorems
5.4
-
5.7
for the first, resp. second, summand on the right-hand side of the above inequality.
∎

Now, we can also provide a version of the Strong Law of Large Numbers with respect to the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
–convergence for the solutions of the mean-field systems.
However, notice that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
the vector
\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}=(Y^{1,N}_{t},...,Y^{N,N}_{t})
consists of random variables which are neither independent nor exchangeable.

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
t\in[0,T]
and consider any set of assumptions of Theorems
5.4
-
5.7
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}Y^{i,N}_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}_{t}]\Big|^{2}\bigg]=0.

Proof.

Direct computations yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}Y^{i,N}_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}_{t}]\bigg|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(Y^{i,N}_{t}-Y^{i}_{t})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(Y^{i}_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}_{t}])\bigg|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{N}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\big|Y^{i,N}_{t}-Y^{i}_{t}\big|^{2}\bigg]+\frac{2}{N^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\big|Y^{i}_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}_{t}]\big|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|Y^{i,N}_{t}-Y^{i}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]+\frac{2}{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|Y^{1}_{t}-\mathbb{E}[Y^{1}_{t}]\big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|Y^{i,N}_{t}-Y^{i}_{t}\big|^{2}\Big]+\frac{2}{N}\mathbb{E}\big[\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\big|Y^{1}_{t}\big|^{2}\big];

in the first inequality we used the well-known inequality
\big(\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\big)^{2}\leq N\sum_{i=1}^{N}a^{2}_{i}
in conjunction with
Remark

5.1
(iii)
.
Applying
Step 2
of the proofs of Theorems
5.4
-
5.7
allows us to conclude.
∎

5.5.
Rates of convergence in the classical setting

Let us consider now the setting of the previous subsection, where we have established a propagation of chaos result for players (or particles) that satisfy BSDEs.
We are now interested in deriving convergence rates for this result.
These rates will be based on the celebrated work of
Fournier and Guillin [29]
and some additional results presented below.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
5.6
hold; moreover, assume there exists a function
R:\mathbb{N}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
with
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}R(N)=0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathscr{N}}\bigg\{\|\xi^{i,N}-\xi^{i}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathcal{F}^{\mathscr{N}}_{T},A;\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}g^{i,N}_{s}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}\Big]\bigg\}\leq R(N).

The proof of Theorem
5.6
reveals that the solutions
\overline{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
of the
N-
player mean-field BSDE (
4.1.1
) and the solutions
\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{N}
of the first
N
McKean–Vlasov BSDEs (
1
) satisfy the following inequalities

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}\big\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{m,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{m}\big\|^{2}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$},\hat{\beta},\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},A,\mathbf{X}^{m}}\leq\frac{3M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}C_{\xi}}{\hat{\beta}(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}R(N)\\
+\frac{6M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[W_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\left(L^{N}\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s}\right)\right)\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s},\end{multlined}

and, for
i\in\mathscr{N}
,

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\big\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\big\|^{2}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$},\hat{\beta},\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},A,\mathbf{X}^{i}}\leq\frac{\big(3M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}C_{\xi}\big)}{\hat{\beta}\big(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}R(N)\\
+\frac{6M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}\big(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[W_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\left(L^{N}\left(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{\textrm{mod},N}_{s}\right),\mathcal{L}\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{\textrm{mod},1}_{s}\right)\right)\right]\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}.\end{multlined}

Therefore, in order to apply
Fournier and Guillin [29, Theorem 1]
and derive convergence rates for the propagation of chaos results in the present setting, we need to know the finiteness of the quantities
\Lambda^{\textbf{S}}_{q,T}
and
\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}
defined below; see
Corollary

5.15
for the justification.
Obviously, one may directly extract analogous estimates for the equations (

MF
Inst

¯
Y

).

Definition 5.11
.

Let
q>2
be a real number and
T\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
be deterministic.
Then, define

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\textbf{S}}_{q,T}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\hat{\beta}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[|Y^{1}_{s}|^{q}+\|Z^{1}_{s}c_{s}\|^{q}+|\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{s}|^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}

and

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\hat{\beta}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[|Y^{1}_{s}|^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}.

Remark 5.12
.

Using
Remark

5.1
.
(iii)
, for all
s\in[0,T]
, we have that
\mathbb{E}\big[|Y^{i}_{s}|^{q}\big]=\mathbb{E}\big[|Y^{j}_{s}|^{q}\big]
, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover, we obviously have that

\|\alpha Y^{1}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{H}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}(\mathbb{G},A,C;\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\frac{1}{\hat{\beta}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[|Y^{1}_{s}|^{2}\right]\mathrm{d}\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A\big)_{s}\leq\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}.

Theorem 5.13
.

Let
\Lambda^{\textbf{S}}_{q,T}<\infty
for some
q>2
and deterministic
T
.
Then, under the assumptions of Theorem
5.6
, there exists a constant
C_{d,p,q,2}>0
, depending on
d,p,q,2
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\big\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\big\|^{2}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$},\hat{\beta},\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},A,\mathbf{X}^{i}}\\
\leq\frac{\big(3M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}C_{\xi}\big)}{\hat{\beta}\big(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}R(N)+\frac{6M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}\big(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}\Lambda^{\textbf{S}}_{q,T}\hskip 2.84544ptC_{d,p,q,2}\times\\
\times\begin{cases}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}},&\text{if}\hskip 5.69046pt(2+p)d<4\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptq\neq 4\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log(1+N)+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}},&\text{if}\hskip 5.69046pt(2+p)d=4\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptq\neq 4\\
N^{-\frac{2}{d}}+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}},&\text{if}\hskip 5.69046pt(2+p)d>4.\end{cases}\end{multlined}

Proof.

Immediate from (
5.5
) and
Fournier and Guillin [29, Theorem 1]
.
∎

Using an inequality analogous to (
5.5
) in case the dependence in the measures stems only from the
Y-
part of the solution, we also have the following result.

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}<\infty
for some
q>2
and deterministic
T
.
Then, under the assumptions of Theorem
5.7
, there exists a constant
C_{d,q,2}>0
, depending on
d,q,2
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\big\|\overline{\mathbf{S}}^{i,N}-\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}^{i}\big\|^{2}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$},\hat{\beta},\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},A,\mathbf{X}^{i}}\\
\leq\frac{\big(3M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}C_{\xi}\big)}{\hat{\beta}\big(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}R(N)+\frac{6M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}\big(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}\hskip 2.84544ptC_{d,q,2}\times\\
\times\begin{cases}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}},&\text{if}\hskip 5.69046ptd<4\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptq\neq 4\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log(1+N)+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}},&\text{if}\hskip 5.69046ptd=4\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptq\neq 4\\
N^{-\frac{2}{d}}+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}},&\text{if}\hskip 5.69046ptd>4.\end{cases}\end{multlined}

The next result provides sufficient conditions for controlling the quantities of interest in (
5.5
) and (
5.5
), and thus to derive convergence rates for the propagation of chaos results.
These conditions can be easily checked, in contrast to the boundedness assumption on
\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}
, however they are stronger and more restrictive;
see also
Remark

5.16
.
(i)
for further discussion in that direction.

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[|Y^{1}_{t}|^{q}\right]<\infty
for some
q>2
and deterministic
T<\infty
.
Then, under the assumptions of Theorem
5.7
, there exists a constant
C_{d,q,2}>0
, depending on
d,q,2
, such that for every
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}({Y}^{1}_{t})\big)\right]\\
\leq\frac{6M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})+2\hat{\beta}C_{\xi}}{\hat{\beta}(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}R(N)+\left(\frac{12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})}{\hat{\beta}(1-12M^{\Phi}_{\scalebox{0.4}{$\blacksquare$}}(\hat{\beta})\big)}\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}+2\left(\mathbb{E}\left[|Y^{1}_{t}|^{q}\right]\right)^{\frac{2}{q}}\right)\hskip 2.84544ptC_{d,q,2}\times\\
\times\begin{cases}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}}&,\text{if}\hskip 5.69046ptd<4\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptq\neq 4\\
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log(1+N)+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}}&,\text{if}\hskip 5.69046ptd=4\hskip 5.69046pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69046ptq\neq 4\\
N^{-\frac{2}{d}}+N^{-\frac{q-2}{q}}&,\text{if}\hskip 5.69046ptd>4.\end{cases}\end{multlined}

Proof.

By definition,
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[|Y^{1}_{t}|^{q}\right]<\infty
and
T<\infty
implies
\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}<\infty
.
Using the triangle inequality for the Wasserstein distance and (
2.31
),
we have

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}({Y}^{1}_{t})\big)\\
\leq 2\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}(\overline{\mathbb{Y}}^{N}_{t}),L^{N}({\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}}^{N}_{t})\big)+2\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}({\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}}^{N}_{t}),\mathcal{L}({Y}^{1}_{t})\big)\\
\leq 2\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}|Y^{m,N}_{t}-Y^{m}_{t}|^{2}+2\mathcal{W}_{2,|\cdot|}^{2}\big(L^{N}\big({\widetilde{\mathbb{Y}}}^{N}_{t}\big),\mathcal{L}\big(Y^{1}_{t}\big)\big).\end{multlined}

Hence, from the analog of (
5.5
) in case the dependence in the measure stems only from the
Y-
part of the solution, and
Fournier and Guillin [29, Theorem 1]
we can conclude.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

(i)

Let
q>2
and
T<\infty
be deterministic, then we have from Jensen’s inequality that

\displaystyle|Y^{1}_{t}|^{q}\leq\left(4\hskip 2.84544ptC_{T}+4\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\xi^{1}|^{q}+\left(\int^{T}_{0}\left|f\left(s,Y^{1}_{s},Z^{1}_{s}c_{s},\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1},\Theta)}(U^{1})_{s},\mathcal{L}(Y^{1}_{s})\right)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}C_{s}\right)^{\frac{q}{2}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t}\right].

Hence, we can satisfy the requirement
\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}<\infty
by an appropriate boundedness condition on
f
and an advanced integrability condition on
\xi^{1}
.
We leave the problem of finding optimal conditions for the requirements
\Lambda^{\textbf{S}}_{q,T},\Lambda^{\textbf{Y}}_{q,T}<\infty
open for future research.
Let us also point out that
Laurière and Tangpi [45]
provide sufficient conditions for deriving rates of convergence in the case of BSDEs driven by Brownian motions.

(ii)

The proofs of
Theorem

5.4
and
Theorem

5.6
allow us to deduce that in order to
derive convergence rates for the path-dependent BSDEs one would
be required to analyze the convergence rates of the empirical measure for random variables on the path space with respect to the Wasserstein distance, in the spirit of
Fournier and Guillin [29]
.

Appendix A
Auxiliary results

A.1.
Construction of a space satisfying
(PC1)

In this subsection, we will show that there exists a space satisfying
(PC1)
. Since we discuss about a sequence of independent identically distributed processes, one naturally expects to construct a countable product space, denoted by
(\Omega,\mathcal{G},\mathbb{P})
, based on a prototype probability space
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{G}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
On this prototype probability space we will construct the pair of martingales
\mathbf{X}^{1}
which satisfies the desired properties.
As one may expect, this is not a condition that is trivially satisfied.
Hence, we are led to consider specific cases, which nevertheless demonstrate the generality of the framework we are using.
Let us recall that a Lévy process is square–integrable if and only if for the corresponding Lévy measure
\nu
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathds{1}_{[1,\infty)}(|x|)|x|^{2}\nu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty.

Example A.1
.

Let
(\Omega^{1},\mathcal{G}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
be the probability space that supports two independent square–integrable Lévy processes, say
(X^{1,\circ},X^{1,\natural})
,which are martingales with respect to their natural filtrations.
We further assume that
X^{1,\natural}
is purely discontinuous.
The independence of the Lévy processes implies the property of having no common jumps; see

Cont and Tankov [22, Proposition 5.3]
.
Hence the desired condition
M_{\mu^{X^{1,\natural}}}[\Delta X^{1,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{1}}]=0
is trivially satisfied.

Let us mention, for completeness, that
[
22
, Proposition 5.3]
refers to Lévy processes with no Gaussian part, but this property remains valid also in the case we describe.

Example A.2
.

Let
({\Omega}^{1},{\mathcal{G}}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
be a probability space that supports a
p
-dimensional, purely discontinuous square–integrable Lévy process
X^{1,\natural}
; for the construction see
Bichteler [7, Theorem 4.6.17]
. By taking product if necessary, we assume that our probability space also supports a sequence of independent random variables,
(h_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, such that the
\sigma-
algebras
\bigvee_{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}}\sigma\big(X^{1,\circ}_{t}\big)
and
\bigvee_{k=1}^{\infty}\sigma\left(h^{k}\right)
are independent and

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[h^{k}]=0,\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\hskip 11.38092pt\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[|h^{k}|^{2}\right]<\infty.

Furthermore, let
(t_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a family of deterministic times indexed in increasing order.
We define

\displaystyle X^{1,\circ}(\omega^{1},t):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}h^{k}(\omega^{1})\mathds{1}_{[t_{k},\infty)}(t).

Then we have that
\mathbf{X}^{1}:=(X^{1,\circ},X^{1,\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We remind the reader that
{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
is the usual augmentation of the natural filtration of the pair. The martingale property for
X^{1,\circ}
comes from (
A.1
). Finally, because Lévy processes are quasi-left-continuous, they jump only at totally inaccessible times. Hence
X^{1,\circ}
and
X^{1,\natural}
have no common jumps and again the condition
M_{\mu^{X^{1,\natural}}}[\Delta X^{1,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{1}}]=0
is trivially satisfied.

Example A.3
.

Consider two independent random walks defined on the same grid.
Then, if we denote as in
Example

A.2
by
(h_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
the jumps of
X^{1,\circ}
,
(\widetilde{h}^{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
the jumps of
X^{1,\natural}
and
(t_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
the grid, by reducing the general case to the one described from a single deterministic time, we have the desired property if the jumps are 0 on average.
That is because
\mathcal{F}^{1}_{t_{k}-}=\left(\bigvee_{\{m\in\mathbb{N}:t_{m}<t_{k}\}}\sigma\big(h^{m},\widetilde{h}^{m}\big)\right)\bigvee\mathcal{N}^{1}
.
Here
\mathcal{N}^{1}
is the
\sigma-
algebra generated from the null sets under
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Note that
X^{1,\natural}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
due to the fact that has finite variation and
He et al. [32, 6.23 Theorem 3)]
.

In the above examples key feature was the concept of independence. We now provide an example which illustrates that independence is not necessary.

Example A.4
.

Let
({\Omega}^{1},{\mathcal{G}}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
be a probability space that supports
h^{1}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{p})
and
h^{2}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathcal{G}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

(A.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[h^{1}\big|\sigma(h^{2})\big]=0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{E}\big[h^{2}\big|\sigma(h^{1})\big]=0.

The relation that is expressed through (
A.2
) is a generalization of independence, when the random variables have zero expectation. Let
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we define

\displaystyle X^{1,\circ}(\omega^{1},t):=\begin{cases}0,\hskip 28.45274pt\text{if}\hskip 2.84544ptt<t_{1}\\
h^{1}(\omega^{1}),\hskip 2.84544pt\text{if}\hskip 2.84544ptt\geq t_{1}\end{cases}\text{and}\hskip 11.38092ptX^{1,\natural}(\omega^{1},t):=\begin{cases}0,\hskip 28.45274pt\text{if}\hskip 2.84544ptt<t_{2}\\
h^{2}(\omega^{1}),\hskip 2.84544pt\text{if}\hskip 2.84544ptt\geq t_{2}.\end{cases}

Then we have that
\mathbf{X}^{1}:=(X^{1,\circ},X^{1,\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
; recall that
{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
is the usual augmentation of the natural filtration of the pair. The martingale property comes from (
A.2
). To see that
X^{1,\natural}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
note that
X^{1,\natural}
has finite variation and use
He et al. [32, 6.23 Theorem 3)]
. Finally, from (
A.2
) and
Cohen and Elliott [21, Lemma 13.3.15 (ii)]
we have
M_{\mu^{X^{\natural,1}}}[\Delta X^{1,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{\mathbb{F}}^{1}}]=0
.

In view of the presented examples, we may assume a canonical space
\Omega^{1}
such that
({\Omega}^{1},{\mathcal{G}}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
is a probability space and
{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
is the usual augmentation of the natural filtration of a pair
\mathbf{X}^{1}:=(X^{1,\circ},X^{1,\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}({\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
(defined on the canonical space
\Omega^{1}
)
, with
M_{\mu^{X^{1,\natural}}}[\Delta X^{1,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{{\mathbb{F}}^{1}}]=0
, where
\mu^{X^{1,\natural}}
is the random measure generated by the jumps of
X^{1,\natural}
. Additionally, let a random variable
\xi^{1}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}_{\hat{\beta}}({\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{T};\mathbb{R}^{d})
for a deterministic time
T
, which will be assumed fixed from now on.

Then, let
\left\{({\Omega}^{i},{\mathcal{G}}^{i},{\mathbb{F}}^{i},\mathbb{P}^{i})\right\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
be copies of the stochastic basis
({\Omega}^{1},{\mathcal{G}}^{1},{\mathbb{F}}^{1},\mathbb{P}^{1})
,
\{\mathbf{X}^{i}:=(X^{i,\circ},X^{i,\natural})\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
the corresponding copies of
(X^{1,\circ},X^{1,\natural})
and
\{\xi^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
the corresponding copies of
\xi^{1}
.
We define the product probability space
\big(\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\Omega}^{i},\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{G}}^{i},\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{i}\big)
.
We denote by
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}
the augmented natural filtration of the pair
\mathbf{X}^{i}
in the product space
\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\Omega}^{i}
under the probability measure
\mathbb{P}:=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{i}
.
Because the pair
\mathbf{X}^{i}
depends only on
{\omega}^{i}
we have

(A.3)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}=\Big(\mathbb{F}^{i}\times\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{i\}}^{\infty}\Omega^{m}\Big)\bigvee\mathcal{N},

where
\mathcal{N}
is the
\sigma
–algebra generated from the subsets of the null sets under the measure
\mathbb{P}
.

Using the methods of
Corollary

A.6
we get that
\mathbf{X}^{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
To prove the identity
M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}[\Delta X^{i,\circ}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}}]=0
we work as in the end of the proof of
Lemma

A.8
.
Let
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of disjoint
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
stopping times that exhausts the jumps of
X^{i,\natural}
and also satisfies the assumptions of
[
21
, Lemma 13.3.15 (
ii
)]
; it is known that such a sequence always exists for every
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
adapted, càdlàg process,
e.g.
, see
[
39
, Definition I.1.30, Proposition I.1.32]
.
Of course, the aforementioned stopping times when viewed in the product space depend only on
\omega^{i}
.
Moreover,
X^{i,\circ}
is also an
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
martingale.
Hence,
\Delta X^{i,\circ}_{\tau_{k}}
will be measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\infty}
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If we denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}
the
\sigma-
algebra of events occurring strictly before the stopping time
\tau_{k}
produced under the filtration
\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}
and with
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}
the respective
\sigma-
algebra under the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{i}
,
then from (
A.3
) we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}=\Big(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}\times\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{i\}}^{\infty}\Omega^{m}\Big)\bigvee\mathcal{N}

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma\big(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}}\big)}=\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\times\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{i\}}^{\infty}\Omega^{m}.

Then, because
\mathcal{N}
is independent from any other sub
\sigma
–algebra of
\overline{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{G}}^{i}}
, where we denoted by
\overline{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{G}}^{i}}
the completion under the measure
\mathbb{P}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\Big[\Delta X^{i,\circ}_{\tau_{k}}\Big|\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\widehat{\sigma\big(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}}\big)}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\Big[\Delta X^{i,\circ}_{\tau_{k}}\Big|\Big(\big(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\big)\times\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{i\}}^{\infty}\Omega^{m}\Big)\bigvee\mathcal{N}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}\left[\Delta X^{i,\circ}_{\tau_{k}}\Bigg|\left(\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\right)\times\prod_{m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{i\}}^{\infty}\Omega^{m}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}^{i}}\left[\Delta X^{i,\circ}_{\tau_{k}}\Bigg|\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\right](\omega^{i})=0,

where we used
[
61
, Section 9.7, Property (k) on p. 88]
in the second equality and

[
21
, Lemma 13.3.15 (
ii
)]
in the last equality.

Moreover, note from (
A.3
) that the sequence
\{\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
consists of independent filtrations of the probability space
\big(\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\Omega}^{i},\overline{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{G}}^{i}},\mathbb{P}\big)
, where we abused notation and denoted the extended measure again with
\mathbb{P}
.

Next, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
define the bimeasurable bijections
g^{i}:\big(\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\Omega}^{i},\overline{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{G}^{i}}\big){}\longrightarrow\big(\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}{\Omega}^{i},\overline{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{G}}^{i}}\big)
by

\displaystyle g^{i}(\omega^{1},\omega^{2},\dots,\omega^{i-1},\omega^{i},\omega^{i+1},\dots):=(\omega^{i},\omega^{2},\dots,\omega^{i-1},\omega^{1},\omega^{i+1},\dots),

i.e.
, the function
g^{i}
switches the places of
{\omega}^{i}
and
\omega^{1}
.
It is easy to check the following properties of the sequence
\{g^{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
:

(i)

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
g^{i}\circ g^{i}=\textrm{Id}_{\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\Omega^{i}}
,
where
\textrm{Id}_{\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\Omega^{i}}
is the identity function.

(ii)

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+},

we have
g^{i}\big(\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{1}_{t}\big)^{-1}=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{t}
,

(iii)

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
and for every
A\in\overline{\bigotimes_{i=1}^{\infty}{\mathcal{G}}^{i}}
,
we have
\mathbb{P}(A)=\mathbb{P}(g^{i}(A)^{-1})
.

(iv)

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\mathbf{X}^{i}:=\mathbf{X}^{1}\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}})
and
\xi^{i}:=\xi^{1}\circ g^{i}
.

Now, for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}}=\sigma\left(\left\{A_{t}\times(t,\infty):t\in\mathbb{R}_{+},A_{t}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{t}\right\}\cup\left\{A_{0}\times\{0\}:A_{0}\in\widehat{\mathcal{F}}^{i}_{0}\right\}\right).

Thus
, from (
A.3
) and
(iii)
above, we have that

\displaystyle Z\in\mathcal{P}^{\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow Z\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}})\in\mathcal{P}^{\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{1}}

and

\displaystyle M\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i};\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow M\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{1};\mathbb{R}^{d}).

From the above properties one can show that

\displaystyle\langle X^{i,\circ}\rangle^{\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i}}=\langle X^{1,\circ}\rangle^{\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{1}}\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}})\hskip 11.38092pt\text{and}\hskip 11.38092pt|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i},X^{i,\natural})}=|I|^{2}*\nu^{(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{1},X^{1,\natural})}\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}).

Hence, from (
2.9
) we have
C^{(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i},\mathbf{X}^{i})}=C^{(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1})}\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}})
and

c^{(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{i},\mathbf{X}^{i})}=c^{(\widehat{\mathbb{F}}^{1},\mathbf{X}^{1})}\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}})
.

Finally, from the existence and uniqueness theorems of
subsection

4.2
, see also
Remark

2.3
, and the fact that
\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A^{i}\big)_{T}=\mathcal{E}\big(\hat{\beta}A^{1}\big)\circ(g^{i},T)
, due to symmetry, we have that for all
i\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle(Y^{i},Z^{i},U^{i}(\cdot),M^{i})=(Y^{1},Z^{1},U^{1}(\cdot),M^{1})\circ(g^{i},\textrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}).

A.2.
Auxiliary results

In this subsection, we will present some useful technical lemmata and their proofs.
Let
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{H}
be filtrations on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{G}^{\circ},\mathbb{P})
such that
\mathbb{G}
is immersed in
\mathbb{H}
and both satisfy the usual conditions.
Special cases of the results presented below appear in
Di Tella [25]
.
Although these are sufficient for our purposes, we present here the more general results for completeness.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
U\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}_{+}
and
g\in\left(\mathcal{G}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})\right)_{+}
.
Consider
C^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}
as defined in (
2.9
) and
K^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}
,
K^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}
as defined in (
2.11
),
for every pair
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Then, we have that

(i)

for
\mathbb{P}\otimes C^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}-a.e.

(\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(\omega,t,x)\hskip 2.84544ptK^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}(\omega,t,\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(\omega,t,x)\hskip 2.84544ptK^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}(\omega,t,\mathrm{d}x).

(ii)

For
\mathbb{P}-a.e.

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}g(\omega,t)\hskip 2.84544ptU(\omega,t,x)\hskip 2.84544pt\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}(\omega,\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x)\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}g(\omega,t)\hskip 2.84544ptU(\omega,t,x)\hskip 2.84544pt\nu^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}(\omega,\mathrm{d}t,\mathrm{d}x).

(iii)

U*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=U*\nu^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
, up to evanescence.

(iv)

\widehat{U}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=\widehat{U}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
, up to evanescence; see (
2.5
) for their definition.

(v)

\zeta^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=\zeta^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
,
up to evanescence; see (
2.7
) for their definition.

Proof.

Let us fix
\mathbf{X}:=(X^{\circ},X^{\natural})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{p})\times\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We remind the reader that the immersion of filtrations implies that
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}=C^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}
; see
Remark

2.1
.
Therefore, we may simplify the notation and simply write
C
for

C^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}
.
We proceed to prove our claims:

(i)

We consider
\{B_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
to be a partition of
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
that makes
M_{\mu^{X^{\natural}}}

\sigma-
integrable with respect to
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{G}}
.
Then, we define the sequence
\{A_{m}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
as
A_{m}:=\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{m}B_{k}\right)\hskip 1.42271pt\cap\hskip 1.42271pt\{|U|\leq m\}
;
of course
\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\nearrow 1
for every
(\omega,t,x)
.
For every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have that
\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\mu^{X^{\natural}}-\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}
is a
\mathbb{G}-
martingale of finite variation,
while
\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\mu^{X^{\natural}}-\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
is an
\mathbb{H}-
martingale of finite variation.
In view of the immersion property,
i.e.
, every
\mathbb{G}-
martingale is also an
\mathbb{H}-
martingale,

\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}-\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
is a predictable,
\mathbb{H}-
martingale of finite variation starting at
0
.
In other words, it is 0 up to indistinguishability for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, which equivalently reads

(A.4)

\displaystyle\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=\left(U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\right)*\nu^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}

up to indistinguishability for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
By (
2.11
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathds{1}_{[0,s]}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(\omega,t,x)\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}(\omega,t,x)\hskip 2.84544ptK^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}(\omega,t,\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}C_{t}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\mathds{1}_{[0,s]}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U(\omega,t,x)\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}(\omega,t,x)\hskip 2.84544ptK^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}(\omega,t,\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}C_{t},

up to evanescence,
for every
s\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Recalling that
\{[0,s]\}_{s\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}}
is a
\pi
-system whose
\lambda-
system produces
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, by an application of Dynkin’s lemma we can replace
[0,s]
in the above equality with any set
D\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
.
Using the monotone convergence theorem, with respect to the sequence
\{\mathds{1}_{A_{m}}\}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
, we get the desired result.

(ii)

Immediate from
(i)
and the disintegration formula (
2.11
).

(iii)

Immediate from (
A.4
) by means of monotone convergence.

(iv)

Immediate from (
A.4
) because from
He et al. [32, 5.27 Theorem, 2) and 11.11 Theorem]
we have by monotone convergence

\displaystyle\widehat{U}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Delta\left((U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}})*\nu^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)

and

\displaystyle\widehat{U}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\rightarrow\infty}\Delta\left((U\mathds{1}_{A_{m}})*\nu^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}\right).

(v)

This is a direct consequence of
(iv)
for
U=1
.

∎

Corollary A.6
.

Let

Z\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G},X^{\circ};\mathbb{R}^{d\times p})
, then

(Z\cdot X^{\circ})^{\mathbb{G}}=(Z\cdot X^{\circ})^{\mathbb{H}}
, up to evanescence.
Moreover, let

U\in G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}})
,
then
U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
, up to evanescence.
In particular, for
\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni x\overset{\textup{Id}}{\longmapsto}x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have that
X^{\natural}=\textup{Id}\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=\textup{Id}\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
.

Proof.

The claim is immediate for the Itō stochastic integrals from their definition, see
Jacod and Shiryaev [39, Definition I.2.1]
, and the fact that
C^{(\mathbb{G},\mathbf{X})}=C^{(\mathbb{H},\mathbf{X})}
.

As for the stochastic integrals with respect to the integer-valued measure
\mu^{X^{\natural}}
, let
U\in G_{2}(\mathbb{G},\mu^{X^{\natural}})
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\|U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\Big|^{2}_{\infty}\Big]

\displaystyle=\|U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{SqIntMartDecomp}}{=}\left\|\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},c)}\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\left\|\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},d)}\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Note that we denoted with

\displaystyle\left(\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},c)},\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},d)}\right)

the unique pair in
\mathcal{H}^{2,c}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\displaystyle U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},c)}+\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},d)}.

Using
He et al. [32, 6.23 Theorem]
we have that

\displaystyle\|U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{G};\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t>0}\left|\Delta\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)_{t}\right|^{2}\right]=\left\|\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},d)}\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\left\|\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\right)^{(\mathbb{H},c)}\right\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})}=0,

and
U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Next, from

Lemma

A.5
.
(iv)

\displaystyle\Delta\big(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}\big)_{t}

\displaystyle=U(\omega,t,\Delta X^{\natural}_{t})\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X^{\natural}\neq 0\}}-\widehat{U}_{t}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}

\displaystyle=U(\omega,t,\Delta X^{\natural}_{t})\mathds{1}_{\{\Delta X^{\natural}\neq 0\}}-\widehat{U}_{t}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}

\displaystyle=\Delta\big(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}\big)_{t}.

Hence, from the above equality, because
U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}-U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,d}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, using again
He et al. [32, 6.23 Theorem]
we conclude that

\displaystyle\|U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}-U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathbb{H};\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t>0}\left|\Delta\left(U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}-U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}\right)_{t}\right|^{2}\right]=0,

thus
U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{G},X^{\natural})}=U\star\widetilde{\mu}^{(\mathbb{H},X^{\natural})}
, up to indistinguishability.

Finally, for
U=\textup{Id}
we get the last claim.
∎

A.3.
Conservation of solutions under immersion of filtrations

In this subsection we will identify the solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs (

MV
Seg

~
S

mod

), (

MV
Seg

Y

), (
4.2.1
), (

MV
Inst

Y

) when we fix all the elements of the data except for the filtrations.

Lemma

A.8
makes precise the previous sentence.

Let us remind the reader of the necessary notation and terminology.
Let us fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and assume
(PC1)
–
(PC7)
, or,
(PC1)
–
(PC3)
and
(PC5)
–
(PC7)
, respectively for each case as in
Section

5
.
For each
i\in\mathscr{N}
, the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs (

MV
Seg

~
S

mod

), (

MV
Seg

Y

), (
4.2.1
), (

MV
Inst

Y

) associated to the data

\big(\mathbf{X}^{i},\mathbb{F}^{i},\Theta,\Gamma,T,\xi^{i},f\big)
under
\hat{\beta}
admit, by Theorems
5.4
–
5.7
, unique solutions, which are denoted by
(Y^{i},Z^{i},U^{i},M^{i})
.

Remark A.7
.

Let
i\in\mathscr{N}
.
Under
(PC1)
,
(PC5)
and
for
U\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathbb{F}^{i},X^{i,\natural};\mathbb{R}^{d})

we have from
Lemma

A.5
that

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{i},\mathbf{X}^{i},\Theta)}(U)=\Gamma^{(\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},\mathbf{X}^{i},\Theta^{i})}(U),\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{P}\otimes C-\text{a.e.}

Lemma A.8

(
Conservation of solutions
)
.

Assume for each case the neccesary assumptions from
Section

5
and fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathscr{N}
.
The unique solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs (

MV
Seg

~
S

mod

), (

MV
Seg

Y

), (
4.2.1
), (

MV
Inst

Y

) associated to the data
\big(\mathbf{X}^{i},\mathbb{F}^{i},\Theta,\Gamma,T,\xi^{i},f\big)
under
\hat{\beta}
, are also the unique solutions of the McKean–Vlasov BSDEs (

MV
Seg

~
S

mod

), (

MV
Seg

Y

), (
4.2.1
), (

MV
Inst

Y

) associated to the data

\big(\mathbf{X}^{i},\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}},\Theta,\Gamma,T,\xi^{i},f\big)
under
\hat{\beta}
.

Proof.

Let us fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\mathscr{N}
.
We are going to prove only the case for (

MV
Seg

Y

); the others follow analogously.
We denote by
(Y^{i},Z^{i},U^{i},M^{i})
the solution of the McKean–Vlasov BSDE (

MV
Seg

Y

) associated to the standard data

\big(\mathbf{X}^{i},\mathbb{F}^{i},\Theta,\Gamma,T,\xi^{i},f\big)
under
\hat{\beta}
.
From
Lemma

A.5
,
Corollary

A.6
,

Remark

A.7
and
Theorem

5.5
we deduce that it will be enough to show that
M^{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbf{X}^{i}}^{\perp_{\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}}})
. From
Proposition

2.2
we will need to check that

(A.5)

\displaystyle\langle X^{i,\circ},M^{i}\rangle^{\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}}=0\quad\text{ and }\quad M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}[\Delta M^{i}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}}]=0.

We remind the reader that
M^{i}\in\mathcal{H}^{2}({\mathbf{X}^{i}}^{\perp_{\mathbb{F}^{i}}})
,
i.e.
,
\langle X^{i,\circ},M^{i}\rangle^{\mathbb{F}^{i}}=0
and
M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}[\Delta M^{i}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{i}}]=0
.

For the first equation in (
A.5
), we have that
X^{i,\circ}M^{i}
remains an
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}-
martingale, since
\mathbb{F}^{i}
is immersed in
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
.
Hence,
\langle X^{i,\circ},M^{i}\rangle^{\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}}=0.

For the second equation in (
A.5
), we are going to use
Cohen and Elliott [21, Lemma 13.3.15 (ii)]
.
The martingale
X^{i,\natural}
is adapted to the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{i}
.
Let
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of disjoint
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
stopping times that exhausts the jumps of
X^{i,\natural}
and also satisfies the assumptions of
[
21
, Lemma 13.3.15 (
ii
)]
; it is known that such a sequence always exists for every
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
adapted, càdlàg process, see
e.g.

Jacod and Shiryaev [39, Definition I.1.30, Proposition I.1.32]
.
Moreover,
M^{i}
is also an
\mathbb{F}^{i}-
martingale.
Hence,
\Delta M^{i}_{\tau_{k}}
will be measurable with respect to
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\infty}
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If we denote by
\mathcal{F}^{\mathscr{N}}_{\tau_{k}-}
the
\sigma-
algebra of events occuring strictly before the stopping time
\tau_{k}
produced under the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
and with
\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}
the respective
\sigma-
algebra under the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{i}
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathscr{N}}_{\tau_{k}-}\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\Big(\bigvee_{m\in\mathscr{N}\setminus\{i\}}\mathcal{F}^{m}_{\infty}\Big)\quad\text{ and}\quad\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\subseteq\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\infty}.

Finally, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta M^{i}_{\tau_{k}}\big|\mathcal{F}^{\mathscr{N}}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta M^{i}_{\tau_{k}}\big|\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\big(\bigvee_{m\in\mathscr{N}\setminus\{i\}}\mathcal{F}^{m}_{\infty}\big)\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\big]\Big|\mathcal{F}^{\mathscr{N}}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta M^{i}_{\tau_{k}}\big|\mathcal{F}^{i}_{\tau_{k-}}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\big]\Big|\mathcal{F}^{\mathscr{N}}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}\big[\Delta M^{i}\big|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{i}}\big](\tau_{k},\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\Big|\mathcal{F}^{\mathscr{N}}_{\tau_{k}-}\bigvee\sigma(\Delta X^{i,\natural}_{\tau_{k}})\Big]

\displaystyle=0,

where we used the tower property for the first equality,

Williams [61, Section 9.7, Property (k) on p. 88]
for the second one,
[
21
, Lemma 13.3.15 (
ii
)]
in the second to last equality, and concluded in view of the known information
M_{\mu^{X^{i,\natural}}}[\Delta M^{i}|\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{F}^{i}}]=0
.
∎

Lemma A.9
.

Let
i\in\mathscr{N}
. The process

\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho_{\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}}^{2}\big(L^{N}\big({\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}|_{[0,\cdot]}^{\text{mod},N}}\big),\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}|_{[0,\cdot]}^{\text{mod},i})\big)
is optional with respect to the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
.

Proof.

Set
\mathbb{X}:=\mathbb{D}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d\times p}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, from
Remark

2.13
the Wasserstein distance of order 2 as a function
\mathcal{W}_{2,\rho_{\mathbb{X}}}:\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X})\times\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X}){}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{+}
is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{X}))
. Next, from
(ii)
of
Remark

4.17
the process
\mathcal{L}(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}|_{[0,\cdot]}^{\text{mod},i})
is predictable. Finally, for the random measure
L^{N}\big({\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}|_{[0,\cdot]}^{\text{mod},N}}\big)
we have that for every
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{X})
the process

\displaystyle L^{N}\big({\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}|_{[0,\cdot]}^{\text{mod},N}}\big)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{m=1}^{N}f(\widetilde{\mathbf{S}}|_{[0,\cdot]}^{\text{mod},m})

is optional with respect to the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{\mathscr{N}}
, hence from (
2.27
) we are done.
∎
